002
FRONT MATTER · WHAT THIS COMPANION IS

What this companion is

This is a working reference for a novel about a Christian empire, kept by the person writing it. Thy Kingdom Come can't be written honestly by someone guessing at scripture, and it can't be written well by someone who has only absorbed the version of scripture that power finds convenient. So the companion does two jobs at once. It teaches the book patiently and from the beginning, as literature, as history, and as an argument that has never stopped arguing with itself. And it keeps a record of what the Empire does to that book once it has the authority to quote it.

It isn't a devotional and it isn't a study guide. Nothing in here asks you to arrive at a settled conclusion about God, and nothing hands you a single meaning for a passage people have fought over for two thousand years. Where a reading is contested, the contest is printed. Where the text is stranger, more violent or more tender than its reputation, that's printed too. You're being handed summaries, analysis, references and tools that send you back to the text itself. Volume One is the world: the scripture the Empire inherited, and the history, institutions, beliefs and places that produced Eden's story. The people and the chapters belong to Volume Two. When a fact about the world matters to one particular person, this volume points to her dossier instead of working it out here. Above all, this is a book you're allowed to ruin. Write in the margins, argue with the entries, cross out anything the drafting proves false, and date the page when you do. The manuscript is the authority here, and this companion serves it. When the two disagree, the manuscript wins and this book gets amended.

"Every empire that has ever quoted a holy book has also decided which parts of it were quotable."
What it isn't
A devotional. A curriculum. A settled theology. An apologetic, in either direction. A book with the right answer at the back.
MARGINALIA
003
FRONT MATTER · HOW TO USE THE COMPANION

How to use it

There's an order to this book, and it's worth following the first time through. The parts move outward from the text itself to the empire that interprets it, and then into the world that empire built: its fall, its institutions, its twelve Dominions and its Trials. You can read a single entry in ten minutes or spend an afternoon on one. Both are legitimate uses.

Work in one of two modes. In study mode you're simply learning, reading an entry with no scene in mind and letting the margins fill with whatever surprises you. In drafting mode you come in with a scene that isn't working, find the entry or the doctrine page that touches it, and take only what you need. The canon register and the logs at the back keep the second mode fast. They're the book's memory, and they only work if you write in them the day you discover something. Discoveries are the real product. If an entry teaches you that a passage the Empire loves is addressed to slaveholders, or that the woman you'd cast as a warning is the only person in her chapter who tells the truth, that's a change to the novel. It belongs in the discovery log at the back, with a date, so that six months from now you can tell the difference between something you decided and something you only absorbed. Nothing here has to be finished. A half-filled page is more useful than a blank one.

The working arc
  1. Learn the book before anyone tells you what it means.
  2. Recognize its people and its patterns on sight.
  3. Watch how power reads it, and what power leaves out.
  4. Build the Empire's religion on purpose, doctrine by doctrine.
  5. Know the world well enough that every room in it has a history.
  6. Apply it to the manuscript, then record what changed.
Two modes
Study
No scene in mind. Read, take notes, follow the cross-references.
Drafting
A scene that resists. Take only what the scene needs, and go.
MARGINALIA
✥ See also: the Canon Register and the Discovery Log, at the back.
004
FRONT MATTER · READING THE PAGE

Reading the page

The layout carries meaning. Once you know the five signals below, you can tell at a glance whether you're reading the text, reading the Empire, reading an argument with no settled answer, or looking at space that belongs to you. The signals hold in black and white, because most of this book will be photocopied at some point.

Information

Plain roman type with no rule beside it. The text and its history, reported as plainly as the material allows. Where the material is disputed, the entry says so.

Scripture
Your desire shall be for your husband, and he shall rule over you.
GENESIS 3.16

Quoted text is italic behind a single fine rule, with the reference beneath it in small tracked capitals.

What the Empire does with it

The heavy frame with the pink edge, and the only one in the book. Anything marked this way is the Empire's reading, not the text's. Someone made that decision, and the novel is free to expose it.

Contested

A broken frame means the companion isn't deciding. Contested always names a real biblical, historical, linguistic or theological dispute among serious readers. It's different from Possible, which marks an idea of your own that you haven't approved.

Yours

Ruled space, on nearly every working page. If it's ruled, it's yours.

✥ Cross-references sit at the foot of the outer column, never inside a sentence, so following one is a choice and not an interruption.

FRONT MATTER · LABELS AND SOURCES

Labels and sources

In the scripture parts (I to IV), unlabeled prose is research: what the text or scholarship says, with scholars named where a position belongs to them. Your own canon carries a source tag and no label. Everything else is labeled at the start of the sentence it governs.

LabelWhat it means
RESEARCHOutside fact, with a source. Unlabeled in Parts I to IV.
INTERPRETATIONA reading of the material, offered as a reading
CONTESTEDA real scholarly or theological dispute
INFERENCEA conclusion drawn from your files that they don't state
POSSIBLEAn idea worth considering, and not canon
OPEN QUESTIONSomething you still have to decide
CONFLICTTwo of your sources disagree
SUPERSEDEDOlder material that no longer controls
DECIDEDA call you've made. Canon from here on.

FRONT MATTER · RULES OF THE HOUSE

Rules of the house

Seven commitments that keep this companion honest. They're the difference between a novel that takes religion seriously and one that uses it as set dressing.

I · The text isn't the Empire
Every entry keeps them apart, because the engine of this novel is the gap between what a passage says and what an institution can make it do.
II · Contested stays contested
Where scholars and traditions genuinely disagree, the disagreement is printed and left standing. The Empire may settle a question. The companion doesn't.
III · No character is an allegory
A biblical parallel can light a character up, and it never maps onto her. Every parallel ends with where it breaks, and the break is usually where the character gets interesting.
IV · Belief has a cost and a teacher
Nobody in this book believes anything for free. Someone taught it to them, and something's at stake in keeping it. Both go in the dossier.
V · Discovery outranks plan
If the research contradicts the outline, the research gets a hearing. The outline is a hypothesis, and this is where it gets tested.
VI · Nothing enters the novel untested
Every borrowed symbol, doctrine and parallel has to answer one question before it earns a scene.
VII · Possible isn't canon
Freewriting, inference and research notes stay marked until you approve them. A compelling idea stays possible until it survives the manuscript, the source files and your own decision.
"Does this serve Thy Kingdom Come, or does it only prove I did the reading?"
The test
Every page in this book answers to the question at the foot of this one.

LEARNING THE BIBLE · BIBLE 101 · I

What the Bible actually is

The first thing to understand, and the thing most people who grew up around this book were never told, is that it's a library. Sixty-six separate documents in the Protestant count, written across something like a thousand years, in three languages, by many hands, for audiences who mostly didn't know the other documents existed. Some of it is law written for a small farming people. Some is court history written to support a dynasty. Some is poetry, some is prophecy shouted at a government, and some is letters from a traveling preacher to congregations he was trying to hold together.

The variety is the most useful fact you can hold onto as a novelist, because it means the Bible has an argument going on inside it. Deuteronomy ties faithfulness to flourishing, and Job gives a righteous man catastrophe and lets his friends make the simple version of that argument until it fails. Ezra sends the foreign wives away, and Ruth makes a foreign woman the great-grandmother of the king. Proverbs says wisdom generally pays, and Ecclesiastes says the pattern doesn't hold. Nobody smoothed these out. They sit in the same volume, and every institution that has used this book has had to choose which side of each argument would be the official one. INTERPRETATION The choosing is your novel. The Empire doesn't have a Bible problem. It has a Bible strategy, and it's the one scriptural empires have usually used: lift the passages about order, hierarchy and submission into the plain and permanent word of God, and file the passages about jubilee, debt release, foreign widows, prophets who insult kings and a messiah executed by the state under poetry or instructions for another time. Nothing has to be deleted. The canon stays intact and the pulpit still cites chapter and verse. All it takes is a consistent policy about which parts are read aloud, and to whom. So read it as a library with an argument in it. When you meet a passage, the useful question is what it says, which side of the internal argument it's on, who benefits from that side winning, and what it would cost a character to have been raised hearing only one half.

"A canon is the list of things a religion has agreed to keep arguing about."
OTNT
The numbers
66 books in the Protestant count · 39 Old, 27 New · Hebrew, Aramaic, Greek · roughly a thousand years of writing · no single author, no single editor, no original title page.
MARGINALIA
✥ See also: How it was assembled · Who gets to say what it means, Part IV.
012
LEARNING THE BIBLE · BIBLE 101 · II

How it was assembled

No angel delivered a table of contents. The collection you can buy in an airport was assembled slowly, by communities, customs and arguments, over centuries, and the process left visible seams. They're worth knowing about, because your Empire will be running the same process on purpose.

On the Hebrew side the Torah settled first, then the prophetic books, then a looser third group, the Writings, which stayed unsettled for a long time. Some books nearly didn't make it. Esther was questioned partly because God is never named in it. Song of Songs was questioned because it's frankly erotic, and it took a great deal of allegory to make it respectable. Ecclesiastes was questioned because it sounds close to faithless. INTERPRETATION The books that struggled were the ones hardest to use as instruction. On the Christian side the process was slower and messier. For the first centuries there was no fixed New Testament, only a widening pool of gospels, letters and apocalypses circulating unevenly between cities. Lists were proposed, disputed and revised. The four gospels that were kept weren't the only ones written. Revelation was long resisted in the Greek-speaking East, and other apocalypses dropped out entirely. Emperors funded copies and convened councils, and Nicaea in 325 wasn't about the canon. By the time Athanasius listed the twenty-seven books in 367, the shortlist was largely settled, by habit as much as by ruling. Two things follow for TKC. INTERPRETATION Whoever can convene, fund, copy and distribute has outsized influence over which texts stay in circulation. And once a canon closes, the arguing doesn't stop. It moves from which books to which verses. The Empire inherits a closed canon, so it does all its work through emphasis, translation, the calendar of readings and commentary, which is quieter editing and much harder to catch.

What the Empire would do with this

POSSIBLE Never touch the canon. Touch the calendar. Decide which passages are read in public worship, which are kept for clergy, which appear in schoolbooks, and which are never printed in the cheap edition sent out to the Dominions.

Books that nearly lost
Esther · Song of Songs · Ecclesiastes · Hebrews · James · 2 Peter · Jude · Revelation
MARGINALIA
✥ See also: The canon timeline, ii.3 · Passages kept quiet, Part IV.
013
LEARNING THE BIBLE · BIBLE 101 · III

The shape of the Old Testament

Thirty-nine books in the Protestant count, and the plot beneath all of them is land: getting it, losing it, and living under someone else's empire on it. Learn the six movements and you'll always know roughly where you're standing.

Torah · Genesis to Deuteronomy
Origins, a family, slavery, escape, and a law given in a desert to a people who don't have a country yet. Everything later quotes or argues with these five books.
Conquest and judges · Joshua to Ruth
Taking the land, then failing to hold together. Judges is the most brutal stretch in the collection and ends with a line that works as a political thesis: everyone did what was right in his own eyes, because there was no king. Ruth answers it with a foreigner.
Monarchy · Samuel to Chronicles
A people demand a king, are warned in detail what a king will take, and get one anyway. Dynasty, temple, civil war and succession by murder follow. Chronicles retells the same reigns with some of the embarrassing material left out.
Exile and return · Ezra to Esther
The state is destroyed and the population deported, and the survivors work out how to be a people without a country. A partial return, a rebuilt wall, a crisis about foreign wives, and a queen in a foreign court in a book that never names God.
Wisdom and poetry · Job to Song of Songs
Where the tradition argues with itself most openly: Job accusing God of injustice, a hymnbook that includes rage, orderly proverbs, a book that calls everything vapor, and a love poem.
Prophets · Isaiah to Malachi
Mostly political speech to the powerful, about idolatry and about the treatment of the poor, the widow, the foreigner and the worker. Some of it looks ahead. Most of it addresses its own moment. This is the section every empire handles most carefully, and the one your resistance will quote.
Contested

Whether the conquest narratives record history, later national memory, or theology told as narrative is genuinely disputed. Your Empire will read them as precedent for holy war, and your dissidents as a small kingdom explaining itself.

GEN–DEUT
JOSH–RUTH
1 SAM–2 CHR

JOB–SONG

LEARNING THE BIBLE · BIBLE 101 · IV

The shape of the New Testament

Twenty-seven documents, all written inside the Roman Empire by members of a movement with no political power, most of them within about seventy years of a public execution. The situation shapes everything, including the parts your Empire will most want to use.

Gospels · Matthew to John
Four accounts of one life, and they don't match, which is the most useful thing about them. Mark is urgent and bleak, Matthew argues that this fulfills the Law, Luke keeps putting women and the poor in the frame, and John does high theology. Read side by side, you're watching four communities decide what mattered. See the Gospel Grid in Part III.
Acts · the movement spreads
A small, property-sharing sect argues its way across the Mediterranean, mostly in rented rooms and courtrooms. Again and again it's hosted by women with houses.
Paul's letters · Romans to Philemon
Occasional letters, and no systematic theology. Each is one side of a conversation with a particular congregation in a particular crisis. Lift a verse out of that and you're quoting an answer while hiding the question.
General letters · Hebrews to Jude
Written to scattered communities under pressure. James is blunt about the rich, and the letters under Peter's name are about living faithfully without power.
Revelation · apocalyptic
Resistance literature in symbols, written for believers under an empire that called itself divine. INTERPRETATION Many scholars read the beast as imperial Rome and Babylon as its capital, and the book as a claim that Rome won't last. Read as a timetable for the far future, it loses its first readers. Read as a banned pamphlet, it's the most politically dangerous book in the collection.
The central irony for TKC

INTERPRETATION Every document here was written by people with no power, about a man the state executed, for readers the state despised. The Empire runs its theology on literature written by the losing side, and the material for its own undoing is bound into the volume it hands out.

MATT–JOHN
ACTS
ROM–PHLM
HEB–JUDE

LEARNING THE BIBLE · BIBLE 101 · V

The genres, and why they matter

Reading a poem as a statute is one of the most common ways this book gets turned into a weapon. A lament isn't a doctrine. A proverb is a general pattern that its own collection contradicts elsewhere. Name what you're holding before you decide what it obliges anyone to do.

Law
Binding instruction for a particular covenant community in a particular economy. Ask who it's addressed to and what it replaced.
Narrative
Stories describe far more than they endorse. The Bible reports polygamy, slavery and massacre without approving them.
Poetry
Compressed, figurative, built on parallel lines. It carries feeling. A great deal of harm done with scripture comes from reading a metaphor flat.
Wisdom
Observed patterns offered as advice, deliberately in tension across books. Proverbs and Ecclesiastes are meant to be read against each other.
Prophecy
Public address, usually to rulers, usually conditional: change this or the consequence follows. Some of it looks far ahead, and most of it speaks to its own day.
Letter
One side of a correspondence about a local crisis. Every general rule you pull out of it has a particular quarrel behind it.
Gospel
Ancient biography with an argument. Each writer selects, orders and shapes to persuade a particular community.
Apocalyptic
Symbolic writing for people under pressure. Its symbols were readable to its first readers and point at the powers of their own day.
The genre test
Before a character quotes anything at anyone: what kind of writing is this, who was it addressed to, and what does the speaker gain by treating it as a rule?
MARGINALIA
✥ See also: How to read a passage in context, vii.
018
LEARNING THE BIBLE · BIBLE 101 · VI

Translation, and which one to trust

Every translation is a set of decisions. Someone had to decide whether a Greek word describing a woman in a congregation means servant or deacon, whether an instruction is addressed to wives or to everyone, and whether an ambiguous phrase gets resolved toward hierarchy or toward mutuality. Most of those decisions are honest attempts with a hard word. Some carry theological commitments, and the committees that make them have confessions, funders and readers in mind.

For your purposes the method is simple. Read any passage that matters in at least two translations from different traditions, and when they diverge, stop, because the divergence is where the interesting work is. POSSIBLE The Empire lives in that gap. An empire that controls the official edition doesn't need to lie. It only needs to keep choosing the harder rendering, generation after generation, until the gentler reading sounds like a modern invention. Translations sit on a spectrum. Some aim at word-for-word fidelity and read stiffly. Some aim at thought-for-thought clarity and smooth over difficulty. Some are paraphrases with a strong voice. None of these is dishonest by definition, but the smoother the English, the more interpretive decisions have already been made for you out of sight. Chapters and verses aren't original either. They were added centuries later for finding passages, and they've made the whole book quotable in fragments, which is exactly the format a state needs for slogans, wall inscriptions and school recitation.

The Empire's edition

POSSIBLE Decide early what the Empire's authorized text is, who may own a different one, what has been footnoted into harmlessness, and which single word in the household passages the Temple's authority rests on. A translation dispute is a perfectly good inciting incident.

Method
Two minimum
Two translations from different traditions. Note every divergence. The gap is the material.
MARGINALIA
✥ See also: Translation, module vi · the household verse, module ix.
019
LEARNING THE BIBLE · READING IN CONTEXT · VII

How to read a passage in context

Eight questions, in this order, every time. Run them and you'll read better than many people who quote this book professionally, and you'll be able to write clergy who are sophisticated and not merely sinister.

I · What kind of text
Name the genre first: law, story, poem, letter, oracle, apocalypse. It decides what kind of claim the passage can make.
II · Who's speaking
The narrator, a character, God as quoted by a character, an opponent being paraphrased, or a friend the book will prove wrong. Job's comforters get pages of confident theology that the book itself rejects (42:7).
III · To whom
A named congregation, a king, a nation, an enslaved population, a household. An instruction to the powerful reads very differently when it's turned on the powerless, and that turn is one of the commonest abuses there is.
IV · What comes before and after
Read the paragraph on either side, then the chapter, then skim the book's argument. A verse used as a wall inscription almost always has a sentence beside it that complicates it.
V · What world produced it
The economy, the legal status of women and slaves, who owned land, what an honor culture punished. The point is to see what the text was arguing with. Sometimes it's more radical than its setting, and sometimes far less.
VI · What the words do
Check a second translation on any word carrying weight. If two respectable versions disagree, you've found a decision and not a fact.
VII · Who gains
Ask it of the passage and of every reading of it, including the ones you like. Interpretations have beneficiaries.
VIII · What it costs
Who has to carry the consequence if this reading is enforced. The answer usually names the character in your novel who lives inside the doctrine.
METHOD
JOB 42.7
EPH 5.21–33

LEARNING THE BIBLE · READING IN CONTEXT · VIII

The household verse

A worked example

Take the passage scriptural empires have built household law on: the instruction to wives in Ephesians. Run the eight questions and see how much of the standard use depends on stopping early. The full treatment is in module ix.

Wives, submit to your own husbands, as to the Lord.
EPHESIANS 5.22, QUOTED ALONE, AS IT APPEARS ON A WALL
Genre
A letter to congregations, not a statute.
The sentence before
"Submitting to one another out of reverence for Christ" (5:21), addressed to everyone. In some of the earliest manuscripts, P46 and Codex Vaticanus among them, 5:22 has no verb of its own and borrows "submitting" from 5:21. Cut 5:21 and a mutual instruction reads as a one-way command.
The passage after
Addressed to husbands and much longer, about 116 Greek words to the wives' 40 by one count. Husbands are told to love three times and to give themselves up as Christ did. They're never told to rule, enforce or correct.
The world
Household codes were a known form. Aristotle's ran master over slave, husband over wife, father over children. Ephesians speaks to wives, children and slaves as moral agents and gives the stronger party duties. INTERPRETATION Read against its setting, it modifies an existing hierarchy. An empire that wants it timeless has to keep the setting out of view.
The words
CONTESTED Whether "head" (kephalē) means authority, source or prominence. The verb hypotassō means to arrange oneself under, and how much weight its middle voice can bear is argued.
Who gains
Whoever is allowed to quote the fragment and forbidden to read the frame.
EPH 5.21–33
COL 3.18–4.1
1 PET 2.18–3.7
P46 · VATICANUS
ARISTOTLE, POLITICS I

What the Empire does

POSSIBLE Prints the fragment, leaves out the mutual line, shortens the husbands' section in the Dominion edition, and sets the whole thing to music so children learn it before they read it.

What this gives TKC

POSSIBLE A scene where a character reads the missing line for the first time. It's a revelation about editing, which is worse than a revelation about God, because it means somebody chose.

023
LEARNING THE BIBLE · CONTESTED READINGS · IX

Contested readings

These are the fault lines. Each has been argued in good faith for centuries by people who know the languages, and no amount of reading settles them. POSSIBLE For the novel they're gifts. A doctrine the Empire has closed is a doctrine still quietly open somewhere in the Empire, and every one of these is a possible conflict between characters.

Headship

Whether the household passages set a permanent order of authority or address one culture's arrangements. Both readings have long histories, and neither is fringe.

Slavery

Whether the letters accommodate an existing institution, quietly undermine it, or endorse it. Both sides of the American abolition fight quoted scripture fluently.

Obedience to rulers

Romans 13 says authorities are appointed by God. The same collection is full of prophets defying kings and apostles refusing magistrates.

Purity and the body

Which purity laws were ritual, which moral, which binding, and who decides. The line drawn here sets what your Empire can regulate about a woman's body and call faith.

Women speaking

One letter silences women in assembly, and the same author greets women leading congregations and names one an apostle. How those fit is a live dispute.

Divine violence

Whether the conquest commands are history, rhetoric, or a later people's theology of their own past. Holy war in your novel rests on which answer the Empire teaches.

Wealth

Whether prosperity signals blessing or is itself the danger. Deuteronomy and the prophets pull in opposite directions, and a ruling class finds the first easier to preach.

The end of things

Whether apocalyptic language describes a coming event, a present reality, or a verdict on a particular empire. A state that teaches the first can promise anything.

TEN PAGES
026
LEARNING THE BIBLE · WHAT IT ACTUALLY IS · 1 OF 4

What the Bible actually is

A library, not a book. Sixty-six documents in the Protestant collection this companion starts from, written across roughly a thousand years, in three languages, by people who didn't know they were contributing to the same volume, and who sometimes argued with each other.

Which Bible this companion uses, and why that needs saying

We start with the sixty-six-book Protestant canon, because it's the Bible in the room where you were raised and the Bible the Empire inherited. It isn't the only one. Jewish tradition holds twenty-four books in three divisions and doesn't arrange them as an Old Testament. Catholic Bibles include the deuterocanonical books. Orthodox collections include those and more, and differ among themselves, and the Ethiopian Orthodox canon is the largest of all. The shape of the collection is itself an argument about what scripture is for.

The internal conversations
The tensionWhat's actually in dispute
Deuteronomy and JobDeuteronomy ties covenant faithfulness to flourishing. Job gives a righteous man catastrophe and lets his friends make the simple version of that argument until it fails. The dispute is over retribution used as a rule.
Ezra and RuthEzra–Nehemiah dissolves foreign marriages as restoration policy. Ruth makes a Moabite woman David's great-grandmother. Two answers to the same question about who belongs.
Proverbs and EcclesiastesProverbs teaches that wisdom can be learned and generally pays. Ecclesiastes watches the same world and reports that the pattern doesn't hold.
Court and prophetThe court histories tell kingship from inside the palace. The prophets address the same kings from outside, about the poor, the widow, the foreigner and the worker, and also about idolatry, false worship and foreign alliances.
The working lens

INTERPRETATION The Bible holds many voices, priestly and prophetic, royal and exilic, addressing different crises. Read these as tensions inside one tradition, not as authors calling each other liars. When a character quotes scripture as if it has one settled opinion, that character is making a choice worth writing about.

027
LEARNING THE BIBLE · THE CANONS COMPARED · 2 OF 4

Four collections

The same core, different edges, a different order, and the order is an argument. Where a collection ends tells you what it thinks the story is about.

CollectionBooksHow it's arrangedWhere it ends
Tanakh (Jewish)24Torah, Prophets (Nevi'im), Writings (Ketuvim). A complete scripture, not an "Old Testament."Chronicles, with Cyrus's decree to go up and rebuild. The story closes on return.
Protestant6639 Old Testament books (the Tanakh's books, counted and ordered differently, with the Prophets moved to the end) and 27 NewMalachi, then Revelation. The Hebrew scriptures end on a promise, which frames them as a prologue.
Catholic73Adds the deuterocanonical books: Tobit, Judith, Wisdom, Sirach, Baruch, 1–2 Maccabees, and additions to Esther and DanielIncludes the Maccabean revolt, armed resistance to an empire that outlawed the faith
Orthodox76 or moreVaries by communion. Greek, Slavonic and Ethiopian traditions differ from each other and from Rome.No single answer. Canon has never been uniform worldwide.
CORRECTION TO THE OLD COMPANION It gave the Protestant New Testament twenty-eight books. It has twenty-seven.
Four words that aren't synonyms

Canon is which books are in. Translation is which words render them. Edition is which notes, headings and apparatus surround them. Lectionary is which passages get read aloud, and when. An empire that can't change the canon can still change the other three, and the lectionary is the cheapest.

What the Empire would do with this page

POSSIBLE Never print it. A citizen who knows there are four collections knows there was a decision, and a citizen who knows there was a decision asks who made it. The Empire's Bible has to feel like weather, not legislation.

PRACTICE
031
LEARNING THE BIBLE · HOW IT WAS ASSEMBLED · 1 OF 6

How it was assembled

There was never a room where the Bible was decided. It's the most useful thing to know about it and the hardest to hold onto, because a room would be easier to write about, and every empire in the book would prefer you believed in one.

Use came first, recognition second

Communities read, copied, argued over and prayed with these texts for generations before anyone published a list. Lists, when they came, mostly ratified habits already formed, and the habits were local.

Four gospels, and what that means

Other gospels existed and circulated. The four in the canon were the ones most widely read, earliest, across the most communities, and treated as apostolic in origin. Irenaeus was arguing for exactly four by about 180, which shows the number was contested enough to need defending. Calling them the gospels that "won" makes it sound like a vote. It was closer to a consensus hardening around what was already in use, with the theological arguments running alongside.

Who actually held the authority

Bishops wrote lists. Councils confirmed regional practice more often than they created it. Emperors funded copies, convened meetings and made some outcomes safer to hold than others. Constantine's role at Nicaea in 325 was real, and Nicaea wasn't about the canon. Local use was the slow current under all of it. Canon formation was a long negotiation among all four, and the balance shifted by century and region.

The claim worth keeping, stated carefully

INTERPRETATION "Whoever can convene the meeting decides the canon" is too absolute, and it credits meetings with more than they did. What holds is that the people who could convene, fund, copy and distribute had outsized influence over which texts stayed in circulation, and circulation is most of what canon means in practice.

Why this sequence is six pages and not one

POSSIBLE The Empire's account of its own Bible will be exactly one page long, and a single page can only give the compressed version.

INFORMATION
IRENAEUS, AGAINST HERESIES 3.11
NICAEA 325
I.2
032
HOW IT WAS ASSEMBLED · THE HEBREW SCRIPTURES · 2 OF 6

Torah, Prophets, Writings

Three divisions, settling at different times. The order they settled in is itself information: the most authoritative material stabilized first, and the edges were still being discussed centuries later.

DivisionRoughly whenWhat was happening
TorahBy the Persian period, about the fifth century BCEWorking as authoritative law for the restored community. Public reading is already an institution, and Nehemiah 8 shows the practice.
ProphetsLater, graduallyThe Former and Latter Prophets circulate as a recognized body. By the second century BCE a writer can refer to "the law and the prophets" as a known pair.
WritingsLast to settleThe loose end. Psalms and Proverbs uncontroversial. Esther, Ecclesiastes and Song of Songs still discussed in the rabbinic period.
SeptuagintThird century BCE onwardThe Greek translation for Greek-speaking Jews, begun in Alexandria, with a wider and looser collection. Most New Testament writers quote it, which is why the Christian Old Testament inherited a different shape.
Early Christian use of Jewish scripture

The first Christians had no New Testament. They had Israel's scriptures, read as testimony about what had just happened to them, which is a reading strategy and not a neutral inheritance. Every appeal to prophecy in the gospels is that strategy at work in public.

The Dead Sea Scrolls, in one paragraph

A library hidden near Qumran and found between 1947 and 1956, with biblical manuscripts about a thousand years older than the standard medieval Hebrew codices. They show a text remarkably stable in most places and genuinely variable in others, which corrects both the idea that the Hebrew Bible never changed and the idea that it was rewritten wholesale.

SIRACH PROLOGUE

DEAD SEA SCROLLS 1947–1956

034
HOW IT WAS ASSEMBLED · THE LONG TIMELINE · 3 OF 6

A thousand years of deciding

Approximate throughout, and contested in places, since dates in this field are arguments and not measurements. What the shape shows is that there was no single moment, and a very long tail.

WhenWhat moved
About the 5th century BCEThe Torah working as authoritative law in the restored Judean community
3rd to 1st century BCEThe Septuagint translated in Alexandria. The Prophets recognized as a body. Wisdom and apocalyptic writing flourish.
About 50–60 CEPaul's letters, the earliest New Testament documents, written to particular congregations before any gospel
About 70–100 CEThe gospels, written after the Temple's destruction in 70. The institution at the center of the story is already rubble when they're written.
2nd centuryMarcion publishes a deliberately reduced collection, and the reaction sharpens everyone's sense that the boundary matters. Irenaeus argues for four gospels.
Date disputedThe Muratorian fragment lists much of what becomes the New Testament and leaves some out. CONTESTED Most date it to the late second century, a minority to the fourth.
3rd–4th centuryEusebius sorts books into accepted, disputed and rejected, a working scholar's triage. Athanasius lists the twenty-seven books in 367.
Late 4th centuryCouncils at Hippo and Carthage in North Africa affirm lists close to today's. Jerome makes the Latin Vulgate and flags books he considers secondary, a distinction Rome didn't adopt.
Middle AgesStable practice in the West. Eastern churches settle differently and not uniformly. Chapter divisions added in the thirteenth century, usually credited to Stephen Langton.
16th centuryReformers separate or drop the deuterocanonical books, and Trent affirms them (1546). Robert Estienne's verse numbers (1551). Printing makes a household Bible possible, and a standardized one enforceable.
19th–20th centuryOlder manuscripts recovered, critical editions built, translations multiply. The text you can buy today rests on more evidence than any earlier century had.
ATHANASIUS, FESTAL LETTER 39 (367)
HIPPO 393 · CARTHAGE 397
TRENT 1546
LANGTON · ESTIENNE 1551

036
HOW IT WAS ASSEMBLED · THE DISPUTED BOOKS · 4 OF 6

The ones they argued about

The tempting theory is that the disputed books were the ones hardest to preach from. It's too tidy. The recorded objections are usually about authorship, origin or particular content, and each book has its own reason. Read the right column as the reasons actually given.

BookStatusWhat the objection was
EstherDiscussedThe Hebrew text never names God, and its festival, Purim, isn't in the Torah. It was received anyway, and the Greek version adds explicitly religious material.
Song of SongsDiscussedExplicit love poetry with no overt theology. Allegory (God and Israel, Christ and the church) became the standard defense, and it remains a reading.
EcclesiastesDiscussedIts conclusions sit awkwardly beside the rest of wisdom literature. Many scholars read its closing verses as a later frame.
HebrewsDisputedAnonymous. The West accepted it more slowly because Pauline authorship couldn't be shown, and nearly all scholars now agree Paul didn't write it.
JamesDisputedSlow to circulate, and later a Reformation flashpoint over faith and works. Luther placed it at the back of his 1522 New Testament. It stayed in.
2 PeterDisputedThe most doubted New Testament book in antiquity, on grounds of style and attribution. Its authorship is still a live question.
JudeDisputedQuotes 1 Enoch, a book outside the canon it ended up in
RevelationDisputedAccepted in the West, long resisted in the Greek East. It's scripture for the Orthodox churches and still isn't read aloud in their liturgy.
The last row is the one to steal

POSSIBLE A book can be fully canonical and never read aloud. It's a real historical arrangement and the most elegant instrument on this page. The Empire never has to ban anything.

HOW IT WAS ASSEMBLED · THE EMPIRE'S INSTRUMENTS · 5 OF 6

Six instruments

The Empire doesn't need to change a word of the canon. Each instrument below is documented somewhere in history, and none requires editing scripture. Rank them by what the Empire would reach for first.

I · The calendar · rank ___
Which feasts exist, which are civic, which are quietly dropped. A calendar decides what a population rehearses every year, and a holiday nobody keeps is a doctrine nobody remembers.
II · The public readings · rank ___
The lectionary, the cheapest and strongest instrument here. For most people, scripture is what gets read aloud. The rest of the book is technically available and functionally silent.
III · Clergy access · rank ___
Who is trained, in what, and who may teach it. Control the training and you don't have to control the pulpit. POSSIBLE The Prelate's assessment team is this instrument with a person's name on it. The Prelate's working name is Prelate Aurelia (FR 33).
IV · The schoolbooks · rank ___
Which stories children learn as stories, and in what order. The slowest instrument and the only one that compounds. A generation taught one shape of the book hears anything else as novelty.
V · Dominion editions · rank ___
Same canon, different apparatus, Dominion by Dominion. Headings, footnotes, cross-references and study notes do most of the interpretive work in any Bible a citizen owns, and none of it is scripture.
VI · Commentary and emphasis · rank ___
What the Broadcast says a passage means, repeated until the gloss can't be separated from the verse. The only instrument that works backward on people who already own the book.
Which instrument the Empire used first, and which one it's still afraid to use

FOURTEEN PAGES
042
LEARNING THE BIBLE · THE GENRE MAP · 1 OF 4

Eight genres

Genre determines what a passage is capable of establishing, and misreading it is the most common way a sincere reader reaches a confident wrong answer.

GenreWhat it's trying to doWhat it can't establish
LawSet a community's obligations, ritual, civil and moral, usually without separating themTimeless policy. Legal codes address one society's cases.
NarrativeTell what happened, shaped for a purpose. It often reports behavior without endorsing it.Approval. A story about polygamy isn't an argument for it.
PoetryMake something felt, through image, parallel lines and intensityDoctrine built on a metaphor read literally
WisdomTeach how the world generally works, and in Job and Ecclesiastes, how often it doesn'tPromises. A proverb is a pattern, not a guarantee.
ProphecyDeliver a message from God to a particular people at a particular moment: indictment, warning, consolation, sometimes prediction. Worship, idolatry and the character of God run throughout.A calendar. Most prophecy speaks to its own audience's present, though some looks ahead and later readers read it forward.
GospelPresent a life so the hearer has to reckon with it. Ancient biography with a purpose.A transcript. Selection and arrangement are part of the form.
LetterAddress a live situation in a named community, often with sustained theological argumentRules without context. You're reading one side of a conversation.
ApocalypticReveal what's really happening behind visible power, in symbols, for people who can't say it plainlyForecasts of technology. Its symbols were readable to its first audience.
What genre error costs

Flattening a metaphor into a rule, or a story into an endorsement, has done real harm and still does. It's one mechanism among several, beside deliberate misuse, selective quotation and institutional interest, and it's worth naming exactly instead of blaming it for everything.

INFORMATION
I.3
043
LEARNING THE BIBLE · THE GENRE MAP · 1 OF 4 · WORKING SPACE
The genre I was taught to read wrong
WRITING
FROM THE FACING PAGE
044
THE GENRES · SMALLER FORMS AND THE TEST · 2 OF 4

Forms inside the forms

Eight genres is the map. These are the units a passage is actually built from, and naming the unit usually settles what it can be used for.

Genealogy
A claim about belonging and legitimacy. Who's included is the argument. Matthew's four women are the point of Matthew's list.
Lament
Structured complaint, often addressed to God and often unresolved. Its place in the canon makes protest a sanctioned form of prayer.
Hymn
Communal praise, often older than the book it sits in, and often quoted because it was already familiar.
Proverb
A compressed observation. Reliable as a pattern, dangerous as a promise, and cruel as a rebuke to someone suffering.
Parable
A story built to move the hearer. It usually turns on one hinge, and allegorizing every detail is how it gets defused.
Oracle
A delivered message with a named addressee, often a foreign nation. Reading an oracle addressed to Babylon as addressed to you is a category error with a long history.
Legal code
Case law with penalties, comparable to other ancient Near Eastern codes. Comparison is where the distinctive parts show.
Court history
A record written near power. Ask who commissioned it and what it leaves out.
The genre test, before quoting anything
  1. What kind of writing is this?
  2. What is this kind of writing for?
  3. What can it establish?
  4. What can't it establish?
  5. Am I using it for the thing it can do?
  6. If not, is the misuse mine, or my character's?

LUKE 15
ISA 13–23

THE EMPIRE
048
LEARNING THE BIBLE · SHAPE OF THE OLD TESTAMENT · 1 OF 5

Six movements

An orientation, not a summary. The collection isn't arranged as one story, and these movements overlap, double back, and in places disagree about what happened.

MovementWhat it's aboutThe question it leaves open
Beginnings · Genesis 1–11Creation, rupture, flood and the scattering of peoples. Two creation accounts sit side by side, never reconciled.Whether human power over others is part of the design or part of the damage
The families · Genesis 12–50A promise attached to one household and carried through four generations of deception, infertility, favoritism and migrationWhy the promise keeps passing through the person who shouldn't have received it
Exodus and law · Exodus–DeuteronomyLiberation from imperial forced labor, forty years of learning to be a people, and a law given before the land is enteredWhether a people freed from empire can make law without reproducing it
Land and kings · Joshua–KingsSettlement, tribal instability, the demand for a king, David's house, two kingdoms and the slow loss of bothWhether monarchy was a concession, a failure or the plan
Exile and return · Ezra, Nehemiah, ChroniclesDeportation, survival under foreign rule, partial return, rebuilding and a fierce argument about who belongsWho counts, who's dissolved out of the community, and who writes the record
Wisdom and prophecy · across the collectionTwo bodies of writing that comment on all the rest, one asking how to live well, one indicting how power behavesWhether either one ever changed anything
This companion's lens, declared

INTERPRETATION Land runs through most of these movements: possession, loss, return, and who may hold it. It's a real and recurring concern of the collection. Reading the whole Old Testament as fundamentally about land is this companion's working lens, chosen because the Empire is a land empire. It isn't a neutral description of the material.

050
SHAPE OF THE OLD TESTAMENT · TWO ORDERINGS · 2 OF 5

Where the story stops

The same books in two sequences, with two different endings, and so two different claims about what the collection is doing.

Tanakh order

Torah, then Prophets, then Writings, closing with Chronicles and Cyrus's decree to go up and rebuild. It ends on return. INTERPRETATION The arc is instruction, then the record of failing it, then the literature of living with that, and the last word is permission to go home.

Christian Old Testament order

Law, history, poetry, prophets, closing with Malachi and a promise of Elijah's return. It ends on anticipation. INTERPRETATION Resequenced this way, the collection reads as unfinished, which makes a New Testament feel like a continuation.

A rough historical spine
WhenWhat happened
About 1000 BCEThe united monarchy of the biblical narrative, David and Solomon. CONTESTED Its scale is actively debated by archaeologists.
About 930 BCEDivision into Israel in the north and Judah in the south. Two kingdoms, two records, one collection.
722 BCEAssyria destroys the northern kingdom. Deportation and dispersal.
587/586 BCEBabylon destroys Jerusalem and the Temple and deports the elite. Much of the collection is written, edited or compiled in the shadow of this.
539 BCEPersia takes Babylon, and return is permitted. Restoration is partial, contested and run under imperial authority.
332–63 BCEGreek, then Roman, rule. Apocalyptic writing develops under occupation.
Which ordering the Empire prints, and what ending it needs its citizens to expect

052
SHAPE OF THE OLD TESTAMENT · FOUR STRONG CLAIMS · 3 OF 5

Say it more carefully

Four claims worth keeping, restated so they survive contact with someone who knows the material. The memorable version is on the left and the defensible version on the right, and the difference between them is the discipline of this companion.

The memorable versionWhat's actually defensible
Judges says there was no king, so the book wants one.The refrain, everyone doing what was right in his own eyes because there was no king, sits in a collection that's divided about monarchy. Samuel records the demand for a king as a rejection of God and lists what a king will take. Judges names the cost of no central authority without settling the argument.
Ruth answers it quietly.INTERPRETATION A good reading. A Moabite woman's loyalty placed in David's genealogy reads as a counterargument to exclusion. The book doesn't announce itself as a rebuttal, and it can't be dated closely enough to prove intent.
Chronicles is the first act of imperial editing in the book itself.Too confident. Chronicles retells Samuel and Kings with visible selection, David's worst material largely absent and worship in the foreground. It's an excellent case of a community re-telling its past for present purposes. Calling it the first, or imperial, overstates what can be shown.
The prophets are about how power treats the poor.They're substantially about that, and reducing them to it loses most of the material: idolatry and false worship, foreign alliances, neighboring nations, ritual, exile and consolation, and the character of God. Keep the social indictment central without letting it become the whole subject.
Why this page exists

A character who makes the left-hand claim can be brilliant and still wrong, and that's useful. A companion that makes it is unreliable, and in two years you won't be able to tell which of your own pages to trust.

058
LEARNING THE BIBLE · SHAPE OF THE NEW TESTAMENT · 1 OF 5

Twenty-seven documents

Written across roughly a century by a movement inside the Roman Empire that had no state power. It wasn't powerless in every sense, and its social position varied by city, decade and class.

The five kinds of document
Gospels · 4
Narrative accounts of Jesus, written decades after the events, each for a particular community with particular concerns
Acts · 1
The movement's spread from Jerusalem outward, by the author of Luke, as the second volume of one work
Pauline letters · 13
Letters to particular congregations about particular problems. Several contain sustained theological argument, and Romans is a worked case.
General letters · 8
Hebrews and the letters attributed to James, Peter, John and Jude, for wider audiences and varied purposes
Apocalypse · 1
Revelation, a genre with its own conventions
Composition order isn't canonical order

Paul's letters come first in time, written in the 50s before any gospel existed. The collection opens with the gospels because it's arranged as a story and not as an archive. Read in the order written, it shows a movement working out what it believed before anyone had written down the life it believed about.

The Roman setting, without flattening it

Occupation, client kings, taxation, crucifixion as public policy, the imperial cult, and a legal status for the movement that changed by place and period. It was tolerated in some cities, prosecuted in others, and not systematically outlawed across the empire for most of this century. Acts shows shared property in Jerusalem specifically, and Paul's letters show congregations with property, patrons and households.

For TKC

INTERPRETATION The useful fact isn't that the early movement was poor or illegal. A religion born under an empire was later administered by one, and the same texts served both.

INFORMATION
ROMAN JUDEA
ACTS 2.44–45
059
SHAPE OF THE NEW TESTAMENT · THE FOUR ACCOUNTS · 2 OF 5

Four accounts, one subject

They differ in selection, arrangement, emphasis and detail, and the differences are the most informative thing about them, because each shows what a particular community needed the story to do.

GospelAboutWhat it emphasizesWhere it begins
Mark65–75Urgency, secrecy, suffering. Shortest, probably earliest, and the source most scholars think Matthew and Luke used.An adult at the river. No birth story.
Matthew80–90Fulfillment of Israel's scriptures, teaching in five great blocks, a new Moses. Written for a community deeply engaged with Jewish law.A genealogy with four women in it, one of them Ruth
Luke80–95The poor, women, outsiders and reversal, the powerful pulled down and the hungry filled. Volume one of two.Two pregnancies and a song about thrones being emptied
John90–110Identity and glory. Long discourses, no parables of the familiar kind, a different chronology and mostly its own material.Before creation
Why four matters for a novelist

The canon keeps four versions instead of harmonizing them into one, and that decision shows on every page. INTERPRETATION An empire that wanted one official account had four to choose from and couldn't quietly reduce them, so the next best thing was deciding which one is read at which festival.

Acts, and what it's doing

A narrative of expansion from Jerusalem to Rome, shaped to show the movement's legitimacy and reach. Where Acts and Paul's own letters describe the same events they don't always agree, and historians treat the gap as real.

061
SHAPE OF THE NEW TESTAMENT · THE LETTERS · 3 OF 5

Who wrote what

Authorship is a scholarly question, not a settled fact, and the distinctions matter for TKC, because the letters most often quoted about households and women's silence are among those whose authorship is most disputed.

GroupScholarly viewWhy it matters here
Undisputed Paul · Rom, 1–2 Cor, Gal, Phil, 1 Thess, PhlmBroad agreementHere Paul names women as coworkers, a deacon and an apostle. CONTESTED 1 Corinthians 14:34–35, the silence passage, sits in an undisputed letter, and some manuscripts place it after 14:40, so many scholars think it was added later.
Disputed Paul · Eph, Col, 2 ThessGenuinely dividedEphesians and Colossians carry the household codes. Whether Paul wrote them changes what "the same author" can be claimed to mean.
Pastorals · 1–2 Tim, TitusMost critical scholars: laterThe strictest instructions about women teaching are here. Say "the Pauline letters" and not "Paul" when arguing across this line.
HebrewsAnonymousBy near consensus, it isn't Paul's. A sustained argument about priesthood and sacrifice, directly useful to any state that runs a temple.
Other attributed letters · Jas, 1–2 Pet, 1–3 Jn, JudeVaries. 2 Peter most doubted.Attributing a letter to a famous name was a convention with a range of practices, so flatly asserting or denying authorship is equally unsafe.
The sentence to keep

INTERPRETATION Several of the passages that restrict women most come from letters whose authorship, or whose placement in the text, is most in question. POSSIBLE The Empire would never mention it, because the instruction's authority rests on the name attached.

063
SHAPE OF THE NEW TESTAMENT · REVELATION · 4 OF 5

Four ways to read it

All four are held by serious readers. This companion leans toward the first, and it's still a framework and not a fact about the text. In TKC the reading a character holds is a political position.

I · Resistance literature against Rome

Written for congregations under imperial pressure, using the conventions of apocalyptic to say what couldn't be said plainly. Strong internal support: seven cities, the imperial cult, a city on seven hills, a luxury economy described with contempt. Most historical-critical scholarship works here.

II · A sequence of future events

Read as a program of things still to come. Widely held in popular and some confessional traditions, with enormous cultural influence and a long record of specific predictions failing, which is part of the material too.

III · A cycle, not a timeline

The visions as recurring patterns of empire, judgment and endurance. Explains the repetition, seals, trumpets and bowls covering the same ground three times, better than a sequential reading does.

IV · Liturgy and vision

The book as worship: hymns, thrones, incense and a heavenly assembly, meant to reorder what the hearer thinks is ultimately in charge. The Eastern churches' reluctance to read it aloud sits oddly beside this, and both facts are true.

The two identifications everyone makes

INTERPRETATION The beast as an imperial power and the woman on the beast as a capital city are the readings the evidence best supports, and they're readings. What's nearly beyond argument is narrower: the book's targets are political and economic, and it expects its audience to recognize them.

REV 13
REV 17–18
REV 4–5
EASTERN LECTIONARY

EIGHTEEN PAGES
067
LEARNING THE BIBLE · TRANSLATION · 1 OF 5

Translation, and how to compare it

The old title asked which translation to trust. It's the wrong question, because it implies one exists that never needs checking. The goal is to understand how translation decisions get made, so you can see one being made and say what it did.

Three languages
Hebrew
Most of the Old Testament. Written originally without vowels. The vowel points were added centuries later by the Masoretes, scribes preserving how the text was read aloud, so a consonantal text can sometimes be voiced more than one way, and where it can, translators choose.
Aramaic
Parts of Daniel and Ezra, and the everyday speech of Judea in the first century. Jesus taught in it, and the gospels record him in Greek.
Greek
The New Testament, in Koine, the common Greek of the eastern Mediterranean. It isn't uniform: Mark's is rough and Hebrews' is polished. Translations that flatten every book to one register lose that.
Manuscripts and variants

No original of any biblical book survives. What exists is thousands of copies, and they differ, overwhelmingly in spelling, word order and obvious slips, and occasionally in something substantive. Textual criticism reconstructs the earliest recoverable text from that evidence. A few famous passages are missing from the earliest manuscripts, and reputable Bibles say so in a footnote: the longer ending of Mark (16:9–20), the woman caught in adultery (John 7:53–8:11), and the Trinitarian clause in 1 John 5:7–8.

Where differences actually come from

Most differences between translations aren't ideological. They come from a genuinely ambiguous word, a construction with no clean English equivalent, a choice of register, or a choice between manuscripts. Some are theologically motivated, and those are worth finding, but if you assume every difference is, you won't be able to spot the real ones.

You don't need the languages

You need to know where the decisions live, and to look at more than one Bible when a sentence is doing something to somebody.

INFORMATION
MASORETES
NA28 · UBS5
I.4
068
TRANSLATION · PHILOSOPHY AND APPARATUS · 2 OF 5

The spectrum

It isn't a scale from honest to dishonest. It runs from keeping the structure of the original to keeping its effect on the reader, and every point on it gives something up.

ApproachWhat it prioritizesWhat it gives up
InterlinearWord-for-word correspondence, original order under the lineReadability. A study tool, not a Bible.
Formal ("literal") · KJV, NASB, ESVThe original's grammar and ambiguity, kept even where English strainsClarity in places. An ambiguity preserved is still one you have to resolve.
Middle · NIV, NRSV, CSBAccuracy in natural EnglishSome seams
Functional ("dynamic") · NLT, Good NewsWhat the sentence does, in idiomatic EnglishWhere the original was ambiguous, the translator has already chosen for you
Paraphrase · The MessageImmediate impact, often one author's voiceAny claim to precision. Good for hearing a passage fresh, useless for an argument about wording.
Committee or soloCommittees give consistency, individuals give voice. Committees have institutional homes, traditions, funders and audiences.Neither is neutral, and neither is disqualified. Knowing who made it is part of reading it.
Why the footnotes matter more than the translation you chose

A footnote that says "or," "other manuscripts read," or "Greek uncertain" is the translator telling you a decision happened here. When a verse is being used against someone, check whether the page admits an alternative.

Chapters, verses and headings aren't scripture

Chapter divisions came in the thirteenth century, verse numbers in the sixteenth, and section headings from modern editors. They make the text findable and quotable, and they also impose breaks the author didn't write. Quoting a verse is normal. The question is whether the unit you quoted is doing the work of the unit around it.

NIV · NRSV · CSB

072
TRANSLATION · WHEN TO GO OUTSIDE · 5 OF 5

Four triggers

Comparing English Bibles gets you a long way. These four situations mean stop and find a real source, a lexicon, a commentary or a scholar, before it goes into the novel as a claim.

I · The argument rests on one word

If a whole doctrine hangs on the range of a single term, the range is the question, and English can't answer it. The household verse is in exactly this situation.

II · The translations split along denominational lines

When the pattern of who renders it which way matches the pattern of who teaches what, something besides grammar is operating, and you need to be able to name it exactly.

III · A footnote says the text is uncertain

Take the admission seriously. The manuscripts disagree or the syntax won't resolve, and building a scene on the resolved version is building on a choice.

IV · You're about to say what the original "really" means

The most dangerous sentence available to a non-specialist, and one used with total confidence by people on every side. A character may say it. The companion may not, without a source.

The claims about the original languages I've been repeating without checking
LEXICON
COMMENTARY
BDB · HALOT · BDAG
073
LEARNING THE BIBLE · READING IN CONTEXT · 1 OF 3

Eight questions

Run all eight, in order, before deciding what a passage means. They won't make you an expert, but most confident misuse of scripture happens by skipping one, and skipping is easy to catch in yourself if you have a list.

1 · What kind of text is this?
Genre first, always. It decides what the passage can establish before any other question is worth asking.
2 · Who's speaking?
The narrator, a character, God as quoted, a rival, a friend who turns out to be wrong. Job's comforters are in the Bible and they're mistaken, and the book says so.
3 · To whom?
A congregation with a particular problem, a king, a foreign nation, one person. Applying an oracle addressed to Babylon to yourself is a category error with a long history.
4 · What comes before and after?
Read the paragraph, then the chapter. Most weaponized verses only work once the sentences on either side have been cut.
5 · What world produced it?
Empire, occupation, exile, monarchy, the household economy, the legal status of women and slaves. The setting is often the subject.
6 · What do the words do?
Command, describe, lament, promise, threaten, concede, argue. Compare two translations and see whether they agree about which.
7 · Who gains from this reading?
Ask it of every reading, including the one you prefer. A reading that benefits you isn't wrong because of it, and it's worth checking twice.
8 · What does this reading cost?
And who pays. POSSIBLE The Empire has never asked this one out loud, and Eden eventually can't stop asking it.
What the method buys you

It doesn't buy authority over people who quote this book for a living. Many of them run these questions better than you will. It buys the ability to tell a reading that has done the work from one that skipped a step, including your own, and to say which step.

LEARNING THE BIBLE · THE FAULT-LINE MAP · 1 OF 4

Eight fault lines

The eight places where reading this book divides people, and where the Empire has a doctrine. Research won't produce agreement on any of them, but it reliably rules out weak claims and shows what the argument is actually about.

Fault lineWhat's actually in disputeWhere TKC uses it
HeadshipThe range of one Greek noun, the reach of the mutual-submission verse, and what carries over from a Roman household codeThe Binding. Every household in the Empire.
Women speakingRestriction passages in the Pauline letters set against the undisputed letters, which name women as coworkers, a deacon and an apostle. Authorship and dating carry weight here.The Vowed. Who may teach, and who may only recite.
SlaveryTexts that regulate slavery without abolishing it, and the principle by which a tradition later called it wrong. The verses aren't in doubt. The principle is the argument.Designation and labor tracks. Who can be assigned.
Divine violenceConquest narratives and judgment oracles: history, theological rhetoric, later reflection or ancient war convention, and how any of it fits a God described elsewhere as mercifulThe Fall. Every purge called cleansing.
Obedience to rulersRomans 13 against the prophets, Revelation and the movement's own martyrs. General principle, or advice to a vulnerable congregation in the imperial capital?The Empire's most quoted text. Its whole legitimacy.
WealthWhether the warnings target attachment, structural injustice or wealth itself, and how they sit beside the prosperity language of the wisdom booksCrownward theology. Blessing as evidence of favor.
Purity and the bodyWhich rules are which, and by whose framework. The ritual and moral distinction is a later category applied to the texts, not a label printed in them.Designation exams, the Salt Ceremony, fertility review
The end of thingsApocalyptic read as address to its own moment, as a future program, as a recurring pattern, or as liturgyWhose reading is orthodoxy, and whose is sedition

078
CONTESTED READINGS · FOUR KINDS OF DISAGREEMENT · 2 OF 4

Not all contested alike

The most important distinction in this part. Treating all four as equally open is how a companion becomes useless, and how a well-read person ends up unable to say that something is simply false.

I · Serious scholarly dispute

Specialists with the languages, the manuscripts and the historical training disagree, and each side can state the other's evidence. The range of kephalē. The authorship of Ephesians. Represent both, and don't pick a winner in the companion's own voice.

II · Denominational or traditional disagreement

Communities differ because of their confessions, their governance and inherited practice, more than because the evidence is contested. Who may be ordained. Whether Revelation is read aloud. Name the tradition instead of treating its position as the neutral one.

III · Open theological questions

Questions the text raises and doesn't close, where evidence runs out and judgment begins. What the conquest narratives say about God. Whether Job is ever answered. These stay open honestly, and they're where a novel does its best work.

IV · Unsupported claims

Claims that fail on the evidence: invented etymologies, a word supposedly mistranslated in every Bible, a council that decided something it never discussed. These are wrong, and the companion can say so, while noting that a character may believe one sincerely and build a life on it. The curse of Ham is in this category (Genesis dossier).

The sorting question

Before writing any dispute into a scene, ask which of the four it is. Category I earns two credible characters. Category IV earns one character who's wrong and one who knows it, and that's a different scene entirely.

082
LEARNING THE BIBLE · THE HOUSEHOLD VERSE · 1 OF 6

Ephesians 5

The worked example, because the Empire's household law is built on this passage and because nearly everything hard about reading scripture is in it at once: a disputed author, a supplied verb, a contested word, a Roman legal background, and a verse break in the wrong place.

Where the sentence actually starts

Verse 21 tells the whole congregation to submit to one another. Verse 22 addresses wives. In P46 and Codex Vaticanus, verse 22 has no verb at all, and Clement of Alexandria quotes it the same way, so the instruction to wives borrows the participle of verse 21, and the two verses are one grammatical movement. Codex Sinaiticus, Alexandrinus and most later manuscripts supply a verb, and most English Bibles supply one too, with a section heading printed between the verses. The heading is modern apparatus, not scripture.

CORRECTION TO THE OLD COMPANION It listed Sinaiticus among the verbless witnesses. Sinaiticus has the verb (hypotassesthōsan). The verbless witnesses are P46 and Vaticanus.
What the participle settles, and what it doesn't

The grammatical link isn't in dispute. What it implies is. One reading holds that mutual submission in 21 governs everything after it. Another holds that 21 introduces submission in general and 22 onward specifies its ordered forms. Bruce Metzger's committee preferred the verbless text, and Peter Gurry (New Testament Studies, 2021) argues the verb is original. The grammar alone doesn't decide the meaning, and anyone who says the Greek settles it is overselling, in either direction.

The authorship question

Ephesians is one of the disputed letters. Whether Paul wrote it divides scholars, which affects any argument that runs "the same author who names women as coworkers also said this."

The structural lesson

Quote verse 22 alone and you have a rule for wives. Quote 21 through 33 and you have a passage that gives husbands three times as many verses (nine to three) and about 116 Greek words to the wives' 40 (Mowczko). INTERPRETATION The mechanism isn't mistranslation. It's fragment selection, plus a heading, plus teaching the doctrine before anyone reads the paragraph. Any institution with a lectionary and a catechism can do it without altering a word.

EPH 5.18–6.9
P46 · VATICANUS
SINAITICUS · ALEXANDRINUS
METZGER, TEXTUAL COMMENTARY
GURRY, NTS 2021
I.7
083
THE HOUSEHOLD VERSE · ONE WORD · 2 OF 6

Head

The Greek word is kephalē. Its range in this period is one of the most argued questions in New Testament lexicography, and the dispute is genuine on both sides.

Proposed senseThe case for it, and the strain against it
Authority overWell attested for rulers and governing bodies, and the natural reading if the passage is arranging a hierarchy. Wayne Grudem surveyed 2,336 instances (1985) and argued "source" is rare, and Joseph Fitzmyer (1989) agreed. Strain: the passage defines the husband's headship by self-giving and nourishing, and gives husbands no order anywhere.
Source or originAttested in some contexts, a river's head, a source of supply, and fits the body imagery, where the head supplies and the body grows. Stephen Bedale (1954) began the modern debate with it, and Catherine Kroeger and Philip Payne developed it. Strain: how common this sense was in ordinary first-century use is exactly what's disputed.
PreeminenceForemost, most prominent, first in honor, with no command attached. Richard Cervin's reply to Grudem (1989) is usually grouped here. Strain: honor and authority are hard to separate in a society organized around both.
Metaphor, unresolvedThe writer is working an extended body metaphor and didn't intend an institutional definition, which would explain why readers have pulled incompatible institutions out of it for two thousand years.
The word rendered "submit"

Hypotassō means to arrange oneself under someone. The middle voice is often read as something the subject does herself, and scholars disagree about how much weight that can bear. INTERPRETATION A voluntary act inside a legal system where the alternative is ruin is voluntary only in a limited sense. POSSIBLE The Empire understands this perfectly. It never forces. It makes the alternative unlivable.

What not to do with this page

Don't use it to announce that the Greek has been solved and the hierarchy dissolved. The point is to be able to say which sense a reading assumes and what that assumption costs.

KEPHALĒ
BEDALE 1954
GRUDEM, TRINJ 1985
FITZMYER 1989
CERVIN 1989

085
THE HOUSEHOLD VERSE · THE ROMAN BACKGROUND · 3 OF 6

The household code

Wives, children and slaves, addressed in that order, isn't an invention of this letter. It's a recognizable Greco-Roman form, instruction on managing a household, which was a political unit as much as a domestic one, because the ordered household was understood as the foundation of the ordered state.

What the form assumes

A male head with legal authority over wife, children and enslaved people, the household as an economic enterprise, and the state's interest in its order. Aristotle's Politics (Book I) sets out the three pairs. A first-century reader would recognize the shape at once, and would notice the departures.

Where this version departs from the form

It addresses the subordinate parties directly as moral agents. It gives the husband the longer and harder instruction. It grounds the arrangement in Christ's self-giving and not in nature or the head's prerogative. Whether that amounts to subverting the code, a Christianized version of it, or an accommodation that made the movement socially survivable is a live argument, and all three are held by people who've done the work. David Balch argued that 1 Peter's code helped present the movement as respectable to suspicious outsiders.

The instructions to husbands

Love as Christ loved and gave himself up. Nourish and cherish as your own body. The passage gives husbands no command to rule, enforce or correct. INTERPRETATION A doctrine of headship that produces enforcement is drawing on the noun in verse 23, and not on anything the passage tells husbands to do.

The part nobody quotes at weddings

The same code goes on to enslaved people and their masters (6:5–9). An institution that treats the wives section as timeless and the slavery section as historically conditioned is making an interpretive decision it usually declines to defend. Noticing that is the most useful single move available to a character inside such an institution.

ARISTOTLE, POLITICS I
BALCH, LET WIVES BE SUBMISSIVE (1981)
EPH 5.25–33
EPH 6.5–9

087
THE HOUSEHOLD VERSE · WHO READS IT HOW · 4 OF 6

Four positions

Held by real people with real arguments. Learn to state each one in its own terms before deciding anything. A character who can only caricature the other reading hasn't thought about it yet.

PositionWhat it holds, and on what evidenceIts hardest problem
Ordered roles (complementarian)Distinct and permanent roles: the husband leads, the wife submits, both equal in worth. Rests on the plain sense of headship in the era's usage and on the order of the code itself.Nothing in the passage authorizes enforcement, and verse 21 addresses everyone
Mutual submission (egalitarian)Verse 21 governs the whole section, and the household instructions apply a shared obligation. Rests on the grammar of the participle and the content of the husbands' instructions.The instructions are still asymmetrical, and only wives are told to submit
Historically situatedA first-century congregation taught to live inside an existing legal structure without being destroyed by it. Rests on the household-code parallels and the letter's occasional character.It needs a principle for deciding what carries over to other centuries, and stating that principle is hard
AccommodationA movement softening its radical edge to survive scrutiny. Rests on the trajectory from the undisputed letters toward the Pastorals.It depends on authorship and dating questions that aren't settled

The modern American debate took its current shape in 1987, with the complementarian Danvers Statement and the founding of Christians for Biblical Equality. See Part IV.

Assign one position to each character who has an opinion, then one to a character who's never been told there's more than one

089
THE HOUSEHOLD VERSE · THE EIGHT QUESTIONS, RUN · 5 OF 6

The method, worked

The eight questions, answered on this passage, so you can see a completed run before doing your own.

1 · Kind of text
A letter containing a household code: correspondence, not legislation, with a recognizable moral-instruction form inside it.
2 · Speaker
The letter's author, writing in Paul's name. CONTESTED whether Paul wrote it. It's neither a narrator reporting nor God quoted directly.
3 · Audience
A congregation, or several, in the Roman province of Asia. Wives, husbands, children and enslaved people are all addressed as present and listening.
4 · Before and after
Before: mutual submission, addressed to all. After: husbands, then children and parents, then slaves and masters. The wives' instruction is the shortest unit in the sequence.
5 · The world
Roman legal households with a male head, an economy that included slavery, a state invested in domestic order, and a small movement under intermittent suspicion.
6 · What the words do
Instruct, in some manuscripts through a participle hanging on the previous sentence. One contested noun. The husbands' portion is longer and makes the heavier demand.
7 · Who gains
From the fragment: whoever holds household authority, and any state that needs orderly households. From the whole passage: much less clear, which is why the fragment is what gets printed.
8 · What it costs
Under the fragment reading, a woman's standing depends on a man's character, with no recourse written into the text. Ask who in Aurelia pays that, and what she's told when she says so.
Where my own first run of this passage went wrong
