LEVITICUS · 6 OF 15 · CHAPTERS 18 AND 20
CONTENT NOTE  Sexual prohibitions, incest, capital penalties, and the modern use of two verses against gay people. The page reports the text and the state of the argument and doesn't settle your theology for you.

The two verses and the rest of the list

Chapter 18 is addressed to Israelite men and opens by refusing the practices of Egypt and Canaan. Most of it is forbidden unions with kin, set out from the man's side. A man's own daughter isn't named, which scholars have explained in different ways. Chapter 20 assigns penalties, often death.

What's in the list
Close kin by blood and marriage, a wife's sister as a rival while the wife lives, sex during menstruation, a neighbor's wife, giving children to Molech, lying with a male "as with a woman," and sex with animals, the only verse that makes a woman the one acting.
"Abomination"
Toʿevah covers the whole list. It's used elsewhere for unclean food, dishonest weights and a lying tongue. The English carries a revulsion the Hebrew may not.
The selection test
INTERPRETATION Anyone citing chapter 18 as binding is also citing the menstruation clause, and is standing seven chapters from the debt release. Consistency is a question about the reader.
CONTESTED 18.22 and 20.13What it holdsAssociated with
A general prohibitionAll sexual relations between men, carried forward in the New Testament (Rom 1, 1 Cor 6)Robert Gagnon (2001), and most historic church teaching
A specific actThe phrasing points narrowly to anal intercourse between menSaul Olyan (1994). Milgrom also limited it to that act and to Israelite men in the land.
The Code's own concernsRead inside a system organized around lineage, seed and category boundariesVarious, building on Douglas
ApplicabilityWhether the Holiness Code binds Christians at all, and by what principle some laws are keptThe moral, ceremonial and civil division, and its critics

LEVITICUS · 7 OF 15 · CHAPTER 19, THE NEIGHBOR CHAPTER

The chapter nobody quotes whole

"Love your neighbor as yourself" sits in the middle of a chapter of labor law, harvest law, court procedure and protection for disabled people. In context it's the summary clause of a list of concrete obligations. The chapter opens "You shall be holy, for I the LORD your God am holy" and puts ritual and ethics side by side without ranking them. Revere your mother and father, mother first.

The harvest edge
Don't reap to the border of the field, don't strip the vineyard, leave what falls for the poor and the foreigner. A standing claim on private production, collected by the poor themselves. Ruth gleans under this law.
Wages the same day
A hired worker's pay may not be held overnight. Wage theft is named beside robbery.
Disability
Don't curse the deaf or put a stumbling block before the blind, harms the victim can't see coming or answer. Compare chapter 21, the same book with a different logic.
Courts and speech
No partiality to great or poor, no slander, and don't stand by while your neighbor's blood is shed. Reprove openly instead of hating in your heart. Confrontation is commanded as the alternative to a grudge.
The foreigner
Treated as a native, "and you shall love him as yourself, for you were foreigners in Egypt." The famous command appears twice, and the second time it's about immigrants.
Honest weights
Just balances, weights and measures. Commercial fraud closes the chapter that holds the love command.
An enslaved woman, 19.20–22

Content note. A man who lies with an enslaved woman promised to another man isn't put to death, "because she was not free." There's an inquiry or a penalty (the word is disputed), and he brings a reparation offering. The text says nothing about her.

LEV 19.2–3
LEV 19.9–10
LEV 19.13–18
LEV 19.20–22
LEV 19.33–36
MARK 12.31
RUTH 2

LEVITICUS · 8 OF 15 · CHAPTER 25, THE SUPPRESSED CHAPTER

Land, debt and release

Every seventh year the land rests. In the fiftieth year, by proclamation, holdings go back to the families that lost them and people in debt service go home. The stated ground is ownership. Land isn't sold permanently because it isn't the seller's to sell, "for the land is mine, and you are foreigners and sojourners with me" (25.23).

The sabbath year
No sowing, no reaping, no harvesting the vines. What grows feeds the household, the workers, the hired laborer, the resident foreigner, the livestock and the wild animals alike.
The fiftieth year
Proclaimed with a horn on the Day of Atonement: liberty throughout the land. The economic reset is announced on the day of national confession, which ties the two together on purpose.
Price mechanics
Sale prices are counted by the number of harvests left until the release. What changes hands is a lease of crops more than the land itself. Debt is designed to expire. Houses in walled cities are an exception.
No interest
Take no interest from a poor kinsman, and support him so he can live beside you. One who sells himself is to be treated as a hired worker until the Jubilee.
Redemption duty
A kinsman may and should buy back a relative's land or freedom. Rescue is a family obligation with a legal form, the machinery Ruth runs on.
The boundary in it
The protections are for kin. The same chapter lets Israel buy slaves from the nations and from resident foreigners, pass them on as inheritance and "make them slaves forever" (25.44–46). Print it whenever the Jubilee is praised.
Was it practiced
Sabbath years are attested in the Second Temple period. There's no clear record of a Jubilee being kept. CONTESTED Whether it was enacted law, a utopian program or an ideal invoked now and then.
Why an empire can't circulate this chapter

INTERPRETATION A permanent land market and inheritable debt are the two ways a household gets captured for generations. Chapter 25 forbids both among kin and grounds the ban in the deliverance the Empire also claims. It can't be denied and can't be taught, so it goes to the closed catechism.

LEVITICUS · 10 OF 15 · WHO MAY APPROACH

The body of the official

Chapter 21 limits who may serve at the altar by descent, marriage, mourning and body. A priest with a listed condition may eat the holy food and may not officiate. The rule concerns how the sanctuary presents itself, and it shuts a class of people out of an office because of their bodies. Both halves of that sentence belong on the page.

The list
A man who's blind or lame, has a marked face or a limb too long, a broken foot or hand, a hunched back, dwarfism, a defect of the eye, scabs or crushed testicles. The same word, mum, marks animals unfit for sacrifice. Interpreters note the symmetry between offerer, offering and officiant, which explains the logic without justifying it.
Held against chapter 19
The book that protects the deaf and the blind from harm bars them from the priesthood. It's a real tension inside Leviticus, and the honest move is to name it. Isaiah 56 later promises eunuchs a name in God's house better than sons and daughters.
Marriage and mourning
Limits on whom a priest may marry, tighter for the high priest, and on which dead he may mourn. A priest's daughter who becomes a prostitute is to be burned (21.9). Office reaches into the household and the funeral.
What priests live on
Set portions of the offerings, shared with their households and closed to outsiders. The sanctuary is also a payroll, and the text isn't embarrassed about it.
For Dominion

POSSIBLE An empire choosing candidates for a public religious role has this chapter as precedent for physical screening, and the Trials are that kind of selection. Whether the Sanctum cites Leviticus 21 for its examinations is a plot decision.

What disqualifies a body from service in my world, and who wrote the list

LEVITICUS · 13 OF 15 · WHAT WAS DONE WITH IT

A book used in two directions

Everything here is reception, what people did with Leviticus.

Inside the Bible
The release language of chapter 25 comes back in Isaiah 61, and Luke has Jesus read that passage in the synagogue at Nazareth and say it's fulfilled today (Luke 4.16–21). The Jubilee is quoted as a program.
Jewish practice
Rabbinic Judaism developed the purity, dietary and agricultural laws into living practice and argument, sabbatical-year observance included. Treating Leviticus as a dead text is a Christian habit.
Early Christian use
In Acts 15 the church decided which of these laws applied to non-Jewish believers. Thomas Aquinas later divided the law into moral, ceremonial and judicial precepts, and the Westminster Confession (1646) made the division Reformed doctrine. The text doesn't contain it, and it's how chapter 18 is kept and chapter 25 dropped.
The Liberty Bell
Cast for the Pennsylvania State House in 1752 and recast in 1753 with 25.10 on it. Abolitionists adopted it in the 1830s. The Anti-Slavery Record called it the Liberty Bell in 1835, and a poem in Garrison's Liberator in 1839 spread the name.
Debt campaigns
The Jubilee 2000 campaign for cancelling the debts of the poorest countries took its name and its argument from chapter 25.
Purity as insult
"Unclean" and "leper" have long been used outside the sanctuary system for people a community wants to keep out, by race, disease or sexuality. The most consequential misuse of the book.
CORRECTION TO THE OLD COMPANION The old companion said the word "jubilee" is on the bell. It isn't. The bell reads "Proclaim LIBERTY Throughout all the Land unto all the Inhabitants Thereof," with the reference Lev. XXV. v X. Its debt reading also needed fixing: the Jubilee returns land and frees Israelites in debt service, and the seventh-year debt release is Deuteronomy 15.

It teaches chapters 11 through 20 and shelves 19.9–15 and chapter 25, keeping the machinery of exclusion and dropping the machinery of release. A character who reads the whole book finds the Empire's own scripture arguing against it, a stronger plot device than any invented heresy.

DEEP-DIVE DOSSIER IV · ✥
Deuteronomy

The book that tells a nation how to be a nation, one sanctuary, one archive, a king held under the law and debts that expire, and the most dangerous book in the Torah for an empire to let people read whole.

Why a full dossier

The audit gives it a dossier on three grounds. It's a constitution, it holds the law that limits a king, and it supplies both the enforcement machinery and the conquest language Dominion runs on.

How to work it

Reference is about a third of these pages, and the rest is yours. Three pages carry content notes. Skip them if you need to, and the dossier still works.

SOURCE MARGIN
DEUT 1–34
MT · LXX · 4QDEUT
WEINFELD, DEUTERONOMY 1–11 (AB)
LEVINSON, DEUTERONOMY AND THE HERMENEUTICS OF LEGAL INNOVATION
329
DEUTERONOMY · 1 OF 15 · THE BOOK ON THE RECORD

What it is

Name
English from the Greek deuteronomion, "second law." It's a mistranslation. In 17.18 the king is told to write "a copy of this law," mishneh hattorah hazzot, and the Greek translators rendered it "this second law." Hebrew Devarim, "words."
Place
The fifth and last book of the Torah, set on the plains of Moab on the last day of Moses' life. Nothing happens. The book is speech, and then a death, and a grave no one knows (34.5–6).
Josiah's scroll
In 2 Kings 22–23, during temple repairs under Josiah, about 622 BCE, the high priest finds "the book of the law." The king tears his clothes, sends to the prophet Huldah, who authenticates it, and purges the outlying shrines. W. M. L. de Wette argued in 1805 that the scroll was a form of Deuteronomy. CONTESTED Whether the core was written for the reform, came south after 722 BCE, or was shaped largely in and after the exile. Traditional readers attribute the book to Moses.
Structure
1–4, a retelling of the wilderness. 5–11, the commandments, the Shema and the sermon on loyalty. 12–26, the law code. 27–28, blessings and curses. 29–30, covenant renewal. 31–34, succession, a song, a blessing and a grave.
Genre
A sermon wrapped around a law code, shaped like an ancient treaty between a great king and a subject people.
A warning
The book holds the most quoted verse about loving God and the most brutal commands about conquest, in the same speech, from the same mouth. A presentation that gives you one without the other is selling something.
What the book is actually about

INTERPRETATION Memory as a political instrument. Almost every command comes with a reason that starts "remember that you were a slave." The book's theory is that a people who forget where they came from will build what they escaped.

DEUT 1.1
DEUT 1–11
DEUT 12–26
DEUT 27–34
DEUT 17.18
2 KGS 22–23

DEUTERONOMY · 2 OF 15 · THE SHAPE OF A TREATY

A loyalty oath, reversed

Assyrian and Hittite kings bound subject peoples with documents of a recognizable type. Deuteronomy has the same parts in roughly the same order, with one substitution. The great king is God, and the human monarch is demoted into the text as a regulated official. INTERPRETATION The substitution is the book's central political act.

Treaty partWhat it doesIn Deuteronomy
PreambleNames the sovereign and the setting1.1–5
Historical prologueRecounts what the sovereign has done, so the terms arrive as gratitude. It differs from Exodus and Numbers in places, and the differences are the argument.1.6–4.43
General stipulationExclusive loyalty, called "love." William Moran showed in 1963 that "love" in treaties names political loyalty.5–11, the Shema at 6.4–5
Specific stipulationsThe code: sanctuary, officers, courts, king, priests, prophets, war, property, debt, family, harvest12–26
Deposit and readingStored beside the ark and read aloud every seventh year, at the feast of Booths, to men, women, children and foreigners10.1–5, 31.9–13
WitnessesHeaven and earth, and a song to be memorized4.26, 30.19, 31.19, 32.1
Blessings and cursesEnforcement27–28
Which treaties

Whether the closer model is the Hittite treaties of the second millennium (Mendenhall), which would support an early date, or the Neo-Assyrian treaties of the seventh century (Weinfeld), which fit Josiah's time.

Why the form matters for TKC

INTERPRETATION If you want to build a state religion, this is the template, and the template is neutral. The same seven parts produce a covenant that limits the king or a loyalty oath that makes him a god, depending on who is named in the preamble. Dominion changes one line and keeps the rest.

DEUTERONOMY · 3 OF 15 · CHAPTER 6, AND WHO TEACHES

The household is the school

The book's most famous passage is a loyalty clause followed by a method. Keep these words on your heart, teach them to your children, talk about them sitting in your house and walking on the road, lying down and rising, bind them on your hand and write them on your doorposts and gates. It's a curriculum with a delivery schedule, located in the house more than the sanctuary.

Where teaching sits
With parents, in domestic time, in ordinary conversation. No priest appears, and no building. INTERPRETATION An institution that wants a monopoly on teaching has to get around chapter 6, not cite it.
The child's question
When a child asks what the laws mean, the answer is a story: "We were Pharaoh's slaves in Egypt" (6.20–21). The catechism is historical. The reason for the law is a memory of servitude.
Two Decalogues
Deuteronomy 5 repeats the ten words with real differences. Exodus says "remember" the sabbath and Deuteronomy says "observe." Exodus grounds it in creation, and Deuteronomy in slavery, "that your male and female slave may rest as well as you." Deuteronomy's coveting command puts the wife first, gives her her own verb and adds the neighbor's field.
The prosperity warning
Right after the teaching method: when you live in houses you didn't build and eat from vines you didn't plant, you'll forget (6.10–12). Later, don't say "my power and the might of my hand have gotten me this wealth" (8.17). The book's named enemy is comfort.
Reception
The verses became a fixed recitation in Judaism, and Jesus names them the first commandment (Mark 12.29–30). Both are receptions of Deuteronomy 6.
Who is allowed to teach a child in my world, and what happens in the house instead

DEUTERONOMY · 4 OF 15 · ONE PLACE ONLY

Centralization and its price

Chapter 12 abolishes local altars and requires sacrifice at one chosen place. The single provision reorganizes a society. It moves worship away from the village, concentrates revenue, leaves a class of local priests without altars and turns pilgrimage into infrastructure. The chapter then works through the consequences, which makes it legislation.

The rule
Sacrifice only at "the place the LORD your God will choose." Local shrines are to be destroyed. Jerusalem is never named.
Meat becomes secular
Since you can't slaughter at a local altar any more, ordinary slaughter at home is allowed. Eat meat in your towns, and pour out the blood. A monopoly at the center creates a whole area of life the sanctuary no longer touches.
Tithe into coin
If the journey's too long, sell the tithe, carry the money and buy "whatever you desire, oxen or sheep or wine or strong drink," and eat it there with the household and the Levite. A tax in kind turned into currency for logistical reasons, and the text says so.
The third-year tithe
Every third year the tithe stays in the town for the Levite, the foreigner, the fatherless and the widow. A centralized system with a mandatory local redistribution valve built in.
The displaced priests
Local Levites without altars appear again and again among the dependents "within your gates." A Levite who comes to the central place may serve there equally (18.6–8). In Josiah's reform the shrine priests didn't serve at the Jerusalem altar (2 Kgs 23.9), so the law and the history don't match.
The mechanism worth stealing

POSSIBLE One authorized place, one authorized calendar, and travel required to comply. Attendance becomes traceable, absence becomes evidence, and the road becomes a tax. Dominion doesn't need to invent a Sanctum monopoly. It keeps chapter 12 and loses chapter 14's local tithe.

DEUTERONOMY · 5 OF 15 · CHAPTER 17, THE LAW OF THE KING

The king under the text

INTERPRETATION Six verses, and the most politically radical passage in the Torah. Monarchy is permitted, conditionally, and then stripped. The office is placed under a document.

How the king arrives
By popular demand, "like all the nations around me," then by God's choice, and from among the people, not a foreigner. Kingship is a concession framed as copying the neighbors.
The four prohibitions
Horses, Egypt, wives and treasure. Read as policy: no standing cavalry, no military alliance with Egypt, no network of dynastic marriages, no royal hoard. They're the four instruments of ancient state power.
The copying clause
He writes out the law in a scroll before the priests and keeps it with him. A sovereign who has to copy out the limits on his own office, and reread them, can't claim not to know them.
The stated reason
"That his heart may not be lifted up above his brothers." Neither efficiency nor piety. The reason is anti-hierarchical, and it's written into the clause.
The other offices
Judges, priests and prophets are each limited in the surrounding chapters: no bribes, no partiality, a test for false prophecy, portions in place of estates. Chapter 17 sits inside a scheme that spreads authority out and checks it.
What's missing
No enforcement. Nobody is named who can remove a king who breaks the list, which is the flaw every later empire uses. Solomon breaks all four: horses from Egypt, Pharaoh's daughter and seven hundred wives, and silver "as common as stone."
The central TKC finding

POSSIBLE The Empire's own scripture forbids the Emperor to pile up wealth, weapons and wives and requires him to copy the law by hand. Dominion can't delete it, so it reinterprets it, most likely by declaring the Emperor exempt as the law's author and not its subject. The move is the theological center of the Empire, and it's available to any character who can read.

DEUT 17.14–20
DEUT 16.18–20
DEUT 18.15–22

1 KGS 3.1
1 KGS 10.27–29
1 KGS 11.3

DEUTERONOMY · 6 OF 15 · CHAPTER 15, THE RELEASE

Debt with an expiry date

Every seventh year, release. Debts between Israelites are cancelled, and debt servants go free, and not empty-handed. The master has to load them with livestock, grain and wine, because you were slaves and were brought out. The chapter also closes the obvious loophole: don't refuse a loan because the seventh year is near.

The release
Creditors release what they've lent to a fellow Israelite. A foreigner's debt can still be collected, and the text says so. Print the limit with the promise.
Two sentences that disagree
"There will be no poor among you" (15.4) and "the poor will never cease out of the land" (15.11), a few verses apart. The first is conditional on obedience, and the second is the reason to open your hand. Jesus quotes the second (Mark 14.7).
The hard-heart clause
Don't harden your heart or shut your hand, and don't let your eye be hostile because the release is coming. Reluctance is named as a sin, which means the drafters expected it.

DEUTERONOMY · 6 OF 15 · CHAPTER 15, THE RELEASE, CONTINUED

Debt with an expiry date, continued

Severance
A Hebrew man or woman freed in the seventh year leaves with provisions from the flock, the threshing floor and the winepress. Freedom made survivable. CONTESTED Whether this revises Exodus 21.7, which kept a sold daughter from going free, or addresses a different case.
The one who stays
An awl through the ear at the door, "and to your female slave you shall do the same." Consent is provided for and then made permanent and visible.
Runaways · 23.15–16
An escaped slave who takes refuge with you isn't returned and may live wherever he chooses. The Code of Hammurabi made harboring a runaway a capital offense.
Was it practiced
In Jeremiah 34, Jerusalem frees its slaves under siege and then takes them back, and Jeremiah answers with a "release" to the sword. Nehemiah 5 settles a debt crisis under pressure. The law was invoked, and there's no evidence of it working as designed.
Held beside Leviticus 25

INTERPRETATION Leviticus returns land every fiftieth year, and Deuteronomy cancels debt every seventh. Two instruments aimed at the same failure, the permanent capture of a household. Together they make generational poverty illegal, which is why an empire teaches neither.

How debt passes to a child in my world, and who wrote that rule

DEUTERONOMY · 7 OF 15 · CHAPTERS 7 AND 20
CONTENT NOTE  Commanded killing, the destruction of civilian populations including children, siege warfare and the taking of captive women. The page reports the text and the state of the argument and doesn't resolve it for you.

The commands nobody embroiders

The book that says love the foreigner because you were foreigners also orders the total destruction of seven named peoples. Both are in it, and a companion that prints one and hides the other isn't a companion.

The devoted thing
Ḥerem, often "utterly destroy," marks persons or property removed from ordinary use by destruction. It isn't unique to Israel. The Moabite king Mesha's inscription, about 840 BCE, boasts of devoting an Israelite town to his god and killing seven thousand people.
Two rules of war
A distant city is offered peace first. If it accepts, its people become forced labor. If not, its men are killed and the women, children and livestock taken. The cities of the land itself are to leave nothing alive that breathes. The law is two-tier, with the harsher tier for the nearest neighbors.
The stated reason
Contamination: "they would turn away your sons from following me," and "that they may not teach you." The justification is ideological containment, the one purges have used since. The same chapter forbids marrying these peoples, which assumes they'll still be there.
Exemptions and the trees
Men with a new house, an unharvested vineyard, a fiancée, or fear are sent home before battle. Fruit trees may not be cut down in a siege: "Are the trees in the field human, that they should be besieged by you?" Restraint and annihilation, drafted on the same page.

DEUTERONOMY · 7 OF 15 · CHAPTERS 7 AND 20, CONTINUED

The commands nobody embroiders, continued

The captive woman · 21.10–14
She shaves her head, cuts her nails, puts off her captive's clothes and mourns her father and mother a month before marriage. If he later doesn't want her, he must let her go where she wants and may not sell her, "since you have humbled her." CONTESTED: Mitigation or legitimation. Both readings are serious.
Did it happen
CONTESTED Archaeology doesn't support a sweeping conquest. Kathleen Kenyon's Jericho excavations and the work at Ai found no city of the right period matching the account, and many see Israel emerging gradually in the central highlands. Judges 1 itself lists peoples not driven out. Kenneth Kitchen, James Hoffmeier and others defend more of the narrative. If the commands were written long after as ideology, that explains their origin and doesn't soften the words.
What Dominion does with a divine war command, and which of my characters carried it out

DEUTERONOMY · 8 OF 15 · CHAPTERS 22 AND 24
CONTENT NOTE  Rape law, virginity evidence, capital penalties and divorce, including a clause that compels marriage to an assailant.

Whose property is injured

The sexual and family laws are addressed to men and largely built around damage to a household's standing. Read as an ethic of consent they make no sense. Read as property and lineage law they make a coherent and disturbing system.

The cloth
A husband who claims his bride wasn't a virgin goes before the elders, and her parents bring out the cloth from the wedding night. If his charge is false, he pays a hundred shekels to her father and can never divorce her. If it's true, she's stoned. Her reputation is treated as her father's asset and her body as evidence.
The field and the city
If a betrothed woman lies with another man in the city, both die, on the reasoning that she didn't cry out. In the field only the man dies, because "she cried out and there was no one to rescue her." Geography stands in for testimony.
The fifty shekels
A man who seizes an unbetrothed virgin pays her father fifty shekels, must marry her and can never divorce her. Exodus 22 lets the father refuse the marriage, and Deuteronomy doesn't mention refusal. Naming this plainly is reading the clause.
Divorce
A husband who finds "some indecency" in his wife may write a certificate and send her away. She may remarry, and she can't go back to him. The certificate lets her prove she's free, and only he can start it. The house of Shammai allowed divorce only for unchastity, and the house of Hillel for anything, even a spoiled dish. Jesus is asked about this verse.
What cuts the other way
The same chapters exempt a new husband from war for a year "to be happy with his wife," forbid taking a widow's garment in pledge, and leave harvest for the widow and orphan. Protection and subordination are the same code here.
How to cite it honestly
The Middle Assyrian Laws let a raped virgin's father hand the rapist's own wife over to be raped. Hammurabi executes a man who rapes a betrothed virgin and declares her blameless. "Better than its neighbors" and "good" are different claims, and some neighbors weren't worse.

DEUTERONOMY · 10 OF 15 · CHAPTERS 27 AND 28
CONTENT NOTE  Siege famine and cannibalism, 28.53–57.

Enforcement

Twelve curses are pronounced from Mount Ebal, and the people answer "Amen" to each. Consent to the penalty clause is gathered in public, out loud.

The twelve curses
An idol made in secret, a boundary stone moved, a blind man sent the wrong way, justice perverted for the foreigner, the orphan and the widow. Almost every one names an act done where nobody can see. The curse list handles offenses that slip past the courts.
The asymmetry
Blessings take fourteen verses (28.1–14). Curses take fifty-four (28.15–68), a little under four times as many: disease, siege, deportation, madness, a famine where parents eat their children, and the people shipped back to Egypt for sale with no buyer. INTERPRETATION A document that spends that many words on punishment is telling you what its drafters expected to need.
The Assyrian parallel
Several curses closely match Esarhaddon's Succession Treaty (672 BCE), known from copies at Nimrud and one found at Tell Tayinat in 2009, the sky as bronze and the earth as iron among them. CONTESTED Hans Ulrich Steymans argues 28.20–44 borrows directly. Others think the shared language came through common treaty convention.
Chapter 13
Report and execute even a brother, son, daughter, wife or friend who secretly urges other gods. The treaty makes the same demand about anyone, family included, who speaks against the king.

DEUTERONOMY · 10 OF 15 · CHAPTERS 27 AND 28, CONTINUED

Enforcement, continued

What it does to suffering
If disaster is a penalty clause, every plague, defeat and famine becomes evidence of guilt. The prophets use this logic. Job and parts of Ecclesiastes refuse it. The Bible argues with itself here.
Choose life
Chapter 30 says the command "is not too hard for you, neither is it far off. It is not in heaven," and ends, "I have set before you life and death, blessing and curse. Therefore choose life." It adds a promise of return after exile, written by people who already knew exile.
CORRECTION TO THE OLD COMPANION The old companion said the curses run "more than four times as many" verses as the blessings. Fifty-four to fourteen is just under four times.
The instrument Dominion inherits

POSSIBLE A liturgy in which the population says amen to its own punishment turns obedience into self-administration and any later misfortune into proof of an earlier offense. An empire holding this chapter never has to explain a famine. It only has to ask what the people did.

How my world explains its last disaster, and who was blamed for it

350
DEUTERONOMY · 13 OF 15 · WHAT WAS DONE WITH IT

The book that wrote the rest

Everything here is reception, what people did with Deuteronomy.

A scroll in a wall
A law book found during temple repairs, read to the king and answered with a national purge. Whatever the scroll was, the narrative makes a found document the trigger for a political revolution, the most useful plot mechanism in the Hebrew Bible.
The history it shaped
Martin Noth argued in 1943 that Joshua through Kings form a "Deuteronomistic History," grading every king by this book's standards, chapter 12 above all. What looks like national chronicle is closer to an audit against a statute.
In the gospels
Jesus answers all three temptations with Deuteronomy (8.3, 6.16, 6.13) and names the Shema the first commandment. It's the book he quotes most in argument.
Covenant as politics
John Winthrop closed "A Model of Christian Charity" (1630) by paraphrasing 30.15–20, "Therefore let us choose life," and Puritan covenant theology read New England through the book's terms. The conquest chapters were read as precedent by people arriving on other people's land.
And the other way
Abolitionists cited 23.15–16 against the Fugitive Slave Act of 1850, and 24.7 with Exodus 21.16 against kidnapping and the slave trade. The same volume served the conqueror and the freed in the same century.
Where a found document could overturn something in my manuscript

DEUTERONOMY · 14 OF 15 · DOMINION'S DEUTERONOMY

The half the Empire teaches

POSSIBLE Everything on this page is a TKC construction to test against the manuscript.

What the Sanctum circulatesWhat it keeps in the closed catechism
One sanctuary, one calendar, one authorized place of offeringThe third-year tithe that stays in the town, and the tithe turned to coin and eaten by the household itself
The curses of chapter 28, as the explanation for every plague and defeatThe same covenant makes the sovereign subject to the document, with four named prohibitions
Obedience taught as loyalty to the Sanctum's reading of the lawThe law is read aloud to the whole assembly, women, children and foreigners included, on a fixed schedule
The conquest chapters, as precedent for cleansing a provinceThe runaway-slave clause, the seventh-year release and the ban on interest among kin
Instruction as the Sanctum's office, delivered in its buildingsChapter 6 puts teaching in the house, in the parents' mouths, with no priest present
The argument in one paragraph

INTERPRETATION Dominion keeps Deuteronomy's enforcement and drops its constitution. The curses, the central sanctuary and the war commands stay in general circulation. The law of the king, the release, the household school and the public reading go into the closed catechism. The Empire isn't opposed by a rival scripture. It's opposed by the second half of its own.

THE TORAH AND TKC · 1 OF 5 · GENESIS AND EXODUS IN THE WORLD

Genesis and Exodus in the world

The phases
Phase One belongs to the Temple and is called Genesis, "Who are you?" Phase Two belongs to the Crown and is called Exodus, "How smart are you?" (TR). POSSIBLE The Crown's phase is named for the book about a ruler who loses an argument to two midwives and a labor force.
Eden and the Eve problem
Eden is named for the garden (TL). The Broadcast's name for its narrative against a candidate whose intelligence becomes the story is the Eve problem, the woman "blamed for asking rather than thanked" (TR). INTERPRETATION Genesis 3 doesn't curse the woman or blame her for asking. The blame in the chapter comes from the man (3.12), and the Broadcast's narrative runs his defense.
Genesis 3.16 in the Assessment
The Temple's marriage theology (CR) depends on reading 3.16 as prescription. POSSIBLE One of the three questions the Prelate writes for Eden personally (TR, name changed under FR 33) asks her to explain 3.16, and the grade depends on which reading she gives.
Leah
Eden's younger sister Leah is a Commonwife under Temple review for not conceiving within the Covenant timeline (TL, MEN). In Genesis, Leah is the fertile wife, and the three barren matriarchs are the women the promise runs through. POSSIBLE The Temple's review quotes "be fruitful and multiply" and never mentions Sarah, Rebekah or Rachel.

Genesis and Exodus in the world, continued

Reassignment and Tamar
Miriam was reassigned to another man's household after Thomas left (FR 0). Thomas left voluntarily (FR 3). How reassignment works is deliberately open. POSSIBLE The Temple grounds it in levirate logic (Gen 38, Deut 25.5–10), which in scripture protects a widow's claim, and the Empire uses it to keep an abandoned wife inside the quota.
Hagar and naming
Hagar is the one person in scripture who names God. Celeste gave Eden a second name that's used only in the house (TL). INTERPRETATION Both are names the official record doesn't hold.
Succession
Crest's claim came through survivorship and not birth order, and Dorian is the rightful heir's son (FR 35, 39). Crest is Dorian's uncle (FR 35). POSSIBLE The Temple preaches Jacob over Esau and Judah over Reuben to make the 2061 succession sound like election.
Shiphrah and Puah
POSSIBLE The model for a Vowed officer who quietly doesn't comply. INTERPRETATION Eden's Public Testimony, where every sentence is defensible and the whole is incendiary (TR), is the midwives' answer to Pharaoh at book length.
Exodus 20.12 in the Assessment
Draft Three turns "Honor thy father and thy mother" into a question about reporting an Unbound woman who housed her children without male authority (DR3). Celeste is that woman.

Leviticus and Deuteronomy in the world

Joseph's administration
POSSIBLE Imperial schoolbooks teach Joseph as the model administrator and the Fracture Years as Egypt's famine, with the Coalition as the steward who stored grain. The Restoration Act made rights conditional (TL), and Genesis 47 is the scriptural picture of a people trading freedom for food and thanking the crown.
The curse of Ham
Aurelia was built over Atlanta's Black memory (DOM). OPEN QUESTION Does the Temple teach the curse of Ham? If a character uses it, the narration has to know it's a documented misuse.
Passover and the household
INTERPRETATION Celeste's kitchen table (TL), the Salt Ceremony and the old way of burial (DOM) are household religion of the kind Exodus 12 and Deuteronomy 6 describe, which is why the Temple can't license them.
Salt
Leviticus 2.13 requires "the salt of the covenant" on every grain offering. POSSIBLE The women's Salt Ceremony (DOM) uses the Temple's own covenant salt, and nobody in the Temple has noticed.

THY KINGDOM COME · VOLUME ONE
II
PART TWO · ❖
The People
of the Text
✥
Almost everyone in this book has been flattened into a lesson by somebody who needed them to mean one thing. Go back and find out what they actually did.
i · How to read a person369
ii · The women of the Hebrew Bible370
iii · The women of the New Testament390
iv · The men397
v · Who echoes whom404
THE PEOPLE OF THE TEXT · METHOD

How to read a person

Every profile in this part runs the same fields, and the order matters. It starts with what the person has been reduced to, because that reduction is what you inherited, and it'll keep steering your instincts until it's written down and looked at. The text comes next, only what's on the page, including the parts that are inconvenient for everyone.

After that, the Empire's use of them, because Dominion has a position on each of these people, and a state that quotes scripture is always curating a gallery of approved and cautionary lives. After that come the echoes in your own cast, and most important, where the parallel breaks, because the break is where your character stops being a reference and starts being a person.

Usually reduced to
The received version: the sermon, the flannelgraph, the insult the name has become.
What the text gives
Only what's written, with the silences marked as silences and left unfilled.
What Dominion does
The Empire's official reading, and what it needs that reading to authorize.
TKC echoes
Which of your characters this lights up, and in which scene.
Where it breaks
The required field. No character is an allegory, and the difference is the character.
Questions and notes
Yours. Nothing printed here has any claim on it.

content:

A note on the unnamed
A great many women in this collection have no name: Lot's wife, Jephthah's daughter, the woman of Judges 19, the woman who bled for twelve years, the woman at the well, the woman who anointed him. Who got a name tells you who was doing the recording. The Registry makes the same choices.
People already worked
Sarah, Rebekah, Leah, Dinah, Shiphrah and Puah, and Zipporah are worked in the Genesis and Exodus dossiers and cross-referenced here.
MARGINALIA
✥ See also: Deep dives I and II · the cast-name table at the end of this part.
369
THE PEOPLE OF THE TEXT · WOMEN · I

Eve

Genesis 2–4 · the woman of the garden · the first dialogue in the Bible

Usually reduced to
The woman who brought sin into the world.
What the text gives
Called ishshah, "woman," by the man, and named Ḥawwah, "mother of all living," only after the judgment (3.20). Her conversation with the serpent is the first dialogue in the Bible. She names her sons, and both times she speaks of God: "I have gotten a man with the LORD" (4.1), "God has appointed for me another offspring" (4.25). Genesis 3 doesn't curse her, and the word "sin" doesn't appear in it.
Where the blame came from
Later, and from outside the chapter. Sirach, about 180 BCE: "from a woman sin had its beginning." 1 Timothy 2.14: "Adam was not deceived, but the woman was." Tertullian, about 200 CE: "you are the devil's gateway." Paul's undisputed letters put it on Adam: "sin came into the world through one man" (Rom 5.12).
What Dominion does
POSSIBLE The Broadcast's Eve problem, the woman "blamed for asking rather than thanked" (TR), runs the man's defense from 3.12.
Where it breaks
Eve is never asked what she wanted. Eden is asked constantly, on camera, and graded on the answer.
Questions for the novel
OTORIGINS
Themes
Asking · blame · naming · the first speech
MARGINALIA
✥ See also: Deep dive I, Genesis, pages 2 and 4.
370
THE PEOPLE OF THE TEXT · WOMEN · II

Hagar

Genesis 16 · 21 · Egyptian · enslaved · the only person in scripture who names God

Usually reduced to
A subplot. The servant who caused trouble, the mistake made while waiting on a promise, the woman whose son wasn't chosen.
What the text gives
An enslaved foreign woman handed to her mistress's husband with no recorded consent and no line of dialogue until she's pregnant and being mistreated. She runs once. In the desert she's met, addressed by name, given a promise about her own descendants, and told to go back. When the chosen son is born she's expelled with bread and a skin of water, and she sets the boy under a bush because she can't watch him die. She's the one who names God, El-roi, the God who sees. Nobody else in Genesis does that. She later takes a wife for Ishmael from Egypt, head of her own household.
Afterlives
Paul makes her an allegory of slavery (Gal 4). Islamic tradition honors her as Hajar, and pilgrims at Mecca retrace her run between two hills. Delores Williams built womanist theology on her survival in Sisters in the Wilderness (1993).
What Dominion does
POSSIBLE Makes her a warning about the servant who forgets her place, and cites the order to return as proof God sends women back to the households they flee. Her naming of God isn't taught.
TKC echoes
Any woman whose body is the instrument of someone else's covenant. The candidate who's used and then surplus.
Where it breaks
Hagar is met in the wilderness and survives it twice. If your character runs and no one meets her, that's a different theology, and you should know which one you're writing.
OTCHARACTER
Themes
Surrogacy · exile · being seen · the used body
MARGINALIA
✥ See also: Deep dive I, Genesis, page 8.

THE PEOPLE OF THE TEXT · WOMEN · II · WORKING SPACE

THE PEOPLE OF THE TEXT · WOMEN · III

Tamar

Genesis 38 · twice widowed · defrauded of her claim · declared righteous by the man who wronged her

Usually reduced to
A scandal skipped in public reading. If she's mentioned, she's the woman who pretended to be a prostitute.
What the text gives
Married into a family, widowed, married to the next brother by custom, widowed again, and sent back to her father's house while the family quietly keeps the third son from her. She's left in legal limbo, neither wife nor free, with no court to appeal to. She veils herself, waits on the road her father-in-law will travel, and takes his seal, cord and staff as a pledge. Accused and sentenced to burn, she sends the three items with a message: "Identify whose these are." He says she's more righteous than he is. Her son Perez enters the royal line.
How scripture remembers her
The elders bless Ruth's marriage, "may your house be like the house of Perez, whom Tamar bore to Judah" (Ruth 4.12), and Matthew names her in Jesus' genealogy (1.3).
What Dominion does
POSSIBLE Omits her. There's no usable version of a story in which a woman entraps a patriarch, is vindicated by the record and written into the ancestry of kings.
TKC echoes
Any woman with no legal standing who has to create evidence. She doesn't accuse. She collects.
Where it breaks
Tamar's society had a law on her side that wasn't being enforced. If Dominion's law is against your character, producing evidence won't be enough. She'll have to change the court.
OTCHARACTER
Themes
Legal limbo · evidence · veiling · vindication
MARGINALIA
✥ See also: Deep dive I, Genesis, pages 7 and 8.

374
THE PEOPLE OF THE TEXT · WOMEN · IV

Miriam

Exodus 2 · 15 · Numbers 12 · 20 · Micah 6.4 · the first woman the Torah calls a prophet

What the text gives
Moses' sister, if the unnamed girl at the river is her. Called "the prophet" at the sea, where she leads the women with a hand drum (Exod 15.20–21). She and Aaron challenge Moses' sole authority, and she alone is struck with a skin disease. The camp doesn't move until she's brought back (Num 12). She dies at Kadesh, and the next verse says there was no water (Num 20.1–2). The rabbis built on that the tradition of Miriam's well, which traveled with Israel for her sake. Micah names her with Moses and Aaron as the three God sent.
Her name
Miriam in Greek is Mariam, which is where every Mary in the New Testament gets hers.
What Dominion does
POSSIBLE Teaches Numbers 12 as the lesson on women who speak against ordained authority, and leaves out the song and the well.
TKC echoes
Eden's mother carries her name (TL). Miriam was reassigned to another man's household after Thomas left (FR 0), and Celeste has no proof she's alive (FR 16).
Where it breaks
The biblical camp waits for Miriam. Whether anyone in the Empire waits for this one is the question.
Questions for the novel

EXOD 15.20–21
NUM 12
NUM 20.1–2
MIC 6.4
B. TAʿANIT 9A
TKC: MIRIAM · TL
375
THE PEOPLE OF THE TEXT · WOMEN · V

Deborah

Judges 4–5 · prophet · judge · the problem no empire has solved

Usually reduced to
An exception. The woman God used because no man was available, a reading the text doesn't support anywhere.
What the text gives
Already a prophet and the judge of Israel when we meet her, hearing cases under a palm tree. No emergency is offered to excuse it, and no apology. CONTESTED ʾeshet lappidot can mean "wife of Lappidoth" or "a woman of torches." She summons the commander Barak, who won't go unless she goes with him. She goes, and tells him the glory will go to a woman. It goes to Jael, a civilian who kills the enemy general with a tent peg. The victory song, among the oldest poetry in the Bible, calls her "a mother in Israel" and ends with Sisera's mother at her window imagining her son dividing the spoil, "a womb or two for every man."
What Dominion does
POSSIBLE Emergency doctrine. She's taught as a temporary provision for a lawless age, proof that when men fail God shames them by using a woman. Her authority becomes an insult aimed at men, and stops being a precedent for women.
TKC echoes
The woman with real authority who's constantly called an exception so the rule survives. And the general who'll only act if a woman stands beside him.
Where it breaks
Deborah is never punished, never diminished and never justifies herself. If your character's authority costs her something, the cost is your invention, and worth being honest about.
CORRECTION TO THE OLD COMPANION The old companion said Deborah composed the song. The heading credits "Deborah and Barak" (5.1). The song speaks of her in the first person in places, and who composed it isn't stated.

377
THE PEOPLE OF THE TEXT · WOMEN · VI–VII

Rahab · Ruth

Two foreign women who talk their way into the royal genealogy.

Rahab · Joshua 2, 6
Reduced to: her profession, permanently. A zonah, usually "prostitute" (Josephus calls her an innkeeper). The text: her house is built into the city wall. She hides two spies, lies to the king's men, and gives an assessment of the war more accurate than anything her own city has produced: "I know that the LORD has given you the land." She bargains for her whole household, and the spies swear an oath and tell her to tie a scarlet cord in her window. When the city falls they bring her family out, "and she has lived in Israel to this day." The New Testament remembers her three times. Dominion: POSSIBLE: a conversion story, the fallen woman who chose correctly, never a woman who conducted foreign policy from the wall. Breaks: Rahab's intelligence is believed. Ask whether yours would be.
Ruth · the book of Ruth
Reduced to: a wedding reading. The text: a Moabite widow with no obligation to anyone, who binds herself to her mother-in-law in the most famous vow of devotion in scripture, spoken by a woman to a woman. She gleans as a foreign laborer under the harvest-edge law. Naomi sends her to the threshing floor to uncover Boaz's feet and wait, "and he will tell you what to do." Ruth doesn't wait. She tells him to spread his cloak over her, the same word he'd used for the wings of the God she'd sheltered under. When the son is born, the women say "a son has been born to Naomi," and that Ruth is better to her than seven sons. Deuteronomy 23.3 bars Moabites from the assembly, and the book writes one into the royal line. Dominion: POSSIBLE: the model immigrant who assimilates completely, the threshing floor retold as modesty and the vow given to a husband. Breaks: Ruth's gamble works because one man chose to honor a law he could have ignored.
CORRECTION TO THE OLD COMPANION The old companion said Rahab's deal was "in writing." It's an oath and a scarlet cord, and the text doesn't say her house was left standing. It also said Ruth proposed on Naomi's coaching. Naomi told her Boaz would tell her what to do, and Ruth spoke first.

379
THE PEOPLE OF THE TEXT · WOMEN · VIII–X

Hannah · Abigail · Michal

Three women of the early monarchy, and the three ways the record lets a woman speak to power.

Hannah · 1 Samuel 1–2
One of two wives, provoked for years by the one with children. Her husband asks, "Am I not more to you than ten sons?" She prays at the sanctuary with her lips moving and no sound, and the priest thinks she's drunk. She corrects him. She vows her son to God's service, leaves him at Shiloh after weaning, and brings him a little robe every year. Her song says the LORD raises the poor from the dust and brings down the mighty, and it's the model for Mary's song. It ends with the first mention in the Bible's order of a king as God's "anointed," from a woman praying before Israel has a king.
Abigail · 1 Samuel 25
Married to Nabal, a rich man called harsh. David, an outlaw with six hundred men, asks for provisions in return for having left Nabal's shepherds unharmed. INTERPRETATION Many readers hear a protection payment. Nabal refuses, and David arms four hundred men to kill every male in the house. Abigail loads donkeys with food and rides out without telling her husband. She kneels, takes the blame on herself, and talks David out of blood guilt he'd regret as king. Nabal dies ten days later, and David marries her. The Talmud counts her among seven women prophets.
Michal · 1 Samuel 18–19, 2 Samuel 3, 6
Saul's daughter. The narrator says she loved David, a thing the Hebrew Bible rarely says of a woman. She saves his life by letting him down through a window and putting an idol in his bed. Saul gives her to another man, Palti. David later demands her back as a political condition, and Palti follows her weeping until he's ordered home. When David dances before the ark she despises him, and the chapter ends: "Michal the daughter of Saul had no child to the day of her death."
For TKC

Hannah is House Mother of House Aurem (TL). "Six Trials. Twelve girls. None of them hers" (TL). Abigail is the House's Debate and Protocol Advisor, "pressure in a pretty dress" (WR 30). POSSIBLE Hannah's story runs the biblical one in reverse, a woman deciding whether to give her daughter Naomi to the institution she's served (FR 29).

381
THE PEOPLE OF THE TEXT · WOMEN · XI–XII

Bathsheba · Tamar of the palace

What the court does to a woman it has already harmed.

CONTENT NOTE  Sexual violence and murder.
Bathsheba · 2 Samuel 11–12, 1 Kings 1–2
Reduced to: a temptress on a roof. The text: she's purifying herself after her period, and the text doesn't say where. Every verb in the scene belongs to the king: he saw, he sent, he took. Her only words are "I am pregnant," two words in Hebrew. Her husband is killed on David's written order, carried in his own hand. Nathan's parable makes her the poor man's lamb. Her first child dies. Decades later she works with Nathan to put her son on the throne and sits at his right hand as queen mother. Matthew names her only as "the wife of Uriah." Dominion: POSSIBLE: keeps the roof and drops the verbs. A woman's visible body explains a powerful man's decision, the most useful doctrine a court can have. Breaks: Bathsheba survives into power. Ask what that costs, and what she becomes.
Tamar · 2 Samuel 13
Reduced to: nothing. Most people raised in church have never heard of her. The text: a princess sent by her father's own instruction to her half-brother's sickroom, and raped there. Before it she argues, tells him "such a thing is not done in Israel," and offers a lawful alternative. Afterward she tears the robe worn by the king's virgin daughters, puts ashes on her head and goes away crying aloud. Her full brother tells her to be quiet. Her father is very angry and does nothing. She lives "a desolate woman" in her brother's house, and that's the last line she gets. Absalom later names his own daughter Tamar. Dominion: POSSIBLE: never read aloud. A king burying his daughter's assault to protect the succession isn't survivable material for a dynasty. Breaks: Tamar's outcry changes nothing. If your character's does, you've chosen mercy. Earn it.
CORRECTION TO THE OLD COMPANION The old companion placed Bathsheba bathing "in her own home" and gave her first words as five. The text doesn't give the place, and the words are two in Hebrew.

383
THE PEOPLE OF THE TEXT · WOMEN · XIII–XVI

Jezebel · Athaliah · Huldah · Vashti

Four women with public power, and four ways the record handles it.

Jezebel · 1 Kings 16–21, 2 Kings 9
A Sidonian princess married to Ahab who supports Baal worship and has the LORD's prophets killed. When Naboth won't sell his vineyard, she writes letters in Ahab's name, seals them with his seal and arranges false witnesses so Naboth is executed by correct procedure. The crime is real. Her death comes in 2 Kings 9. She paints her eyes, calls down to the usurper from her window and is thrown out of it. Afterlife: the name became an insult for any woman with influence, and under slavery and Jim Crow "Jezebel" named a stereotype of Black women as sexually predatory, used to excuse their abuse. The insult makes power the sin, more than the murder.
Athaliah · 2 Kings 11
The only woman to rule Judah in her own name, for six years. She takes the throne by killing the royal family, as several male kings took theirs. An infant prince survives because his aunt Jehosheba hides him in the temple. In the seventh year a priest crowns the boy, and Athaliah cries "Treason! Treason!" before she's killed. Her reign is recorded as usurpation, and the same conduct in men as accession.
Huldah · 2 Kings 22
A prophet in Jerusalem. When the law scroll is found in the temple, King Josiah sends the high priest, the royal secretary and three other officials to inquire of the LORD, and they go to her. Jeremiah and Zephaniah were active, and nobody explains why they chose her or remarks that she's a woman. She authenticates the scroll and gives an oracle, and the greatest reform in the kingdom's history follows. Dominion: POSSIBLE: not in the syllabus at any level. The biblical Huldah has no connection to the Prelate, whose working name is Aurelia (POSSIBLE, FR 33).
Vashti · Esther 1
Ordered to appear in her crown before drunk officials, she refuses. The advisers panic about precedent, since every wife will hear of it. She's deposed by decree, and letters go out that every man is to be master in his own house. The plot of the book runs on her empty place.
CORRECTION TO THE OLD COMPANION The old companion cited 1 Kings for Jezebel's death. It's 2 Kings 9. It also said Vashti is "never mentioned again." The king remembers her in 2.1, and Esther is made queen "instead of Vashti."

385
THE PEOPLE OF THE TEXT · WOMEN · XVII–XVIII

Esther · the woman of worth

Esther · the book of Esther
Hadassah, an orphan raised by her cousin, is "taken" in a royal search for young virgins. Twelve months of beauty treatment, six with oil of myrrh and six with spices, then one night with the king. She hides her people on her cousin's instruction. When Haman wins a decree to destroy the Jews, Mordecai says "Who knows whether you have not come to the kingdom for such a time as this?" Approaching the king unsummoned is punishable by death, and she goes: "If I perish, I perish." She works through two banquets, exposes Haman and wins a counter-decree. In chapter 9 the Jews kill their attackers, seventy-five thousand in the provinces. She then writes with full authority to fix Purim, so the book ends with her issuing a decree. God isn't named anywhere in the Hebrew. The Assessment in Draft Three uses "Esther's crown" as proof a woman could influence government "so long as she was beautiful, obedient, and prepared to die for the privilege" (DR3).
The woman of worth · Proverbs 31
An acrostic poem. ʾEshet ḥayil is usually "virtuous" or "capable" wife, and ḥayil also means an army's strength and wealth. She buys a field and plants a vineyard with her own earnings, makes garments and sells them to merchants, "opens her mouth with wisdom," and her husband is known at the gates because of her. The chapter opens with what King Lemuel's mother taught him: stay off wine when judging, and "open your mouth for the mute… defend the rights of the poor and needy." Boaz uses the same phrase for Ruth. Verse 29 is printed on the Assessment and read before the scores (DR3). In Jewish practice it's sung at the Friday evening table.
For TKC

The Trials are a state search for young women with a long preparation (TR). POSSIBLE The Temple teaches Vashti right before Esther as the warning, and never teaches that Esther ends the book issuing a decree in her own name.

THE PEOPLE OF THE TEXT · WOMEN · XIX

The unnamed, and two widows

CONTENT NOTE  The woman of Judges 19, sexual violence and dismemberment. Jephthah's daughter, a child sacrifice.
Lot's wife · Genesis 19
Looks back at Sodom and becomes a pillar of salt. No name.
Jephthah's daughter · Judges 11
Her father vows to sacrifice whatever comes out of his house first if he wins. She comes out dancing. She asks for two months in the mountains with her companions to mourn, he carries out the vow, and "it became a custom" for the daughters of Israel to lament her four days a year. No name.
The woman of Judges 19
A Levite's secondary wife. At Gibeah men surround the house demanding the guest, and the Levite pushes her outside. She's raped all night and found at dawn with her hands on the threshold. He cuts her body into twelve pieces and sends them through Israel. The civil war that follows ends with the abduction of the women of Jabesh-gilead and the dancing girls of Shiloh. "Everyone did what was right in his own eyes." No name.
The prophet's widow · 2 Kings 4
A creditor is about to take her two children as slaves. Elisha tells her to borrow every empty jar, shut the door and pour her one jar of oil. It runs until the jars do. She pays the debt and lives on the rest. The miracle keeps two children out of debt slavery.
The Shunammite · 2 Kings 4, 8
A wealthy woman who builds Elisha a room. When her son dies she rides to the prophet and won't leave without him. Years later she comes home from a famine to find her land taken, petitions the king herself, and gets it back with the income it earned while she was gone.
Which unnamed woman in the Empire's records I'll give a name to

THE PEOPLE OF THE TEXT · WOMEN · XX

Mary of Nazareth

Luke 1–2 · Mark 3 · John 2 · 19 · Acts 1 · a young woman who sings about thrones emptied

Usually reduced to
Serenity. A statue in blue, silent and compliant, the whole personality reduced to one word of consent.
What the text gives
Told she'll bear a son, she asks a practical question, "How will this be?", before she agrees. She goes "with haste" to Elizabeth's house in the hill country and stays three months, and the song she sings there takes Hannah's as its model: God has brought down the mighty from their thrones, filled the hungry and sent the rich away empty, all in the past tense, as though already done. At Cana she tells him there's no wine and tells the servants to do whatever he says. In Mark the family comes to take him home because people say he's out of his mind. She stands at the cross in John and is with the disciples after.
CONTESTED
Matthew quotes Isaiah 7.14 from the Greek, "a virgin shall conceive." The Hebrew ʿalmah means a young woman of marriageable age, and the translation is one of the oldest disputes between Jewish and Christian readers. Luke never gives her age. INFERENCE The usual "teenager" comes from ancient marriage customs.
What Dominion does
POSSIBLE Builds a doctrine of womanhood on her single yes and sets the song so gently nobody hears the words. The revolution she sings is footnoted as spiritual.
Where it breaks
Her consent is asked for. An angel waits for an answer. At an Aurem Binding, "nobody asks if she consents. They ask if she is worthy" (DOM). INTERPRETATION Every Binding held in a Temple that honors Mary contradicts the story it honors.
CORRECTION TO THE OLD COMPANION The old companion said she "travels alone" to Elizabeth's. The text doesn't say whether anyone went with her.

THE PEOPLE OF THE TEXT · WOMEN · XXI–XXII

Mary Magdalene · the woman at the well

Two women sent to speak, and what was said about them afterward.

Mary Magdalene · all four gospels
Reduced to: a prostitute. The text never says so. The text: from Magdala. Seven demons had gone out of her, and she and other women supported Jesus and the twelve "out of their own means." She's at the cross and the burial in all four and named first in most lists of the women. In John she's the first to see the risen Jesus and is sent to tell the disciples, "I have seen the Lord." In Luke the apostles think the women's report is nonsense. Afterlife: Pope Gregory the Great merged her with the unnamed sinful woman of Luke 7 and with Mary of Bethany in a sermon in 591, and it stuck for fourteen centuries. The Catholic calendar dropped the identification in 1969 and in 2016 raised her memorial to a feast with the old title "apostle to the apostles." TKC: POSSIBLE: The Broadcast's Eve narrative "implies and never accuses" (TR), and the reputations it assigns outlast the Trial.
The woman at the well · John 4
Reduced to: her five husbands. The text: a Samaritan woman at noon. Jesus asks her for a drink, and she asks why a Jewish man is asking a Samaritan woman. Their conversation is the longest Jesus has with any one person in the gospels. She raises the live dispute between their peoples, Gerizim or Jerusalem, and gets a serious answer. He says she's had five husbands, and the text doesn't explain or condemn it. She's the first person in John to whom he says plainly he's the Messiah. She leaves her water jar, and "many Samaritans believed because of the woman's testimony." She's never given a name. Breaks: she gets a real answer. Ask who in Dominion would answer her.
CORRECTION TO THE OLD COMPANION The old companion said "women in that world did not divorce men." Under Jewish law only a husband could start a divorce, and Roman law let either spouse. The text doesn't say how her marriages ended.

THE PEOPLE OF THE TEXT · WOMEN · XXIII–XXIV

The bleeding woman, the anointer, Martha and Mary

The woman who bled twelve years · Mark 5
She's spent everything on doctors and gotten worse. She comes up behind him in a crowd and touches his cloak. Under Leviticus 15 she'd count as impure. He stops and asks who touched him, and she comes forward trembling and tells "the whole truth." He calls her "Daughter." The story is set inside the raising of a twelve-year-old girl. INTERPRETATION She takes what she needs without permission and is commended for it.
The woman who anointed him · Mark 14
Two days before his arrest an unnamed woman breaks a jar of costly ointment over his head. The men scold her for the waste, and he says wherever the gospel is preached, what she's done will be told in memory of her. Her name wasn't kept.
Martha and Mary of Bethany · Luke 10, John 11–12
In Luke, Mary sits at his feet, the posture of a disciple, while Martha is "distracted with much serving." He says Mary has chosen the good portion. In John, Martha makes the confession Peter makes in the other gospels: "You are the Christ, the Son of God." Mary anoints his feet and wipes them with her hair.

THE PEOPLE OF THE TEXT · WOMEN · XXV–XXX

The women who ran things

Named, titled and working, in the same letters used to silence women. Any institution has to choose which list is the rule.

Phoebe · Romans 16
A diakonos of the church at Cenchreae, the word translated "deacon" for men, and a prostatis, a patron, "of many and of myself as well." INFERENCE: She carried the letter to Rome, and couriers often read letters aloud and explained them. The KJV and ESV call her a "servant." The NRSV and NIV (2011) say "deacon."
Junia · Romans 16
Greeted with Andronicus as "of note among the apostles," and as Paul's fellow prisoners. Early writers including John Chrysostom read her as a woman apostle. From the Middle Ages many editions printed a masculine "Junias," a name with no ancient attestation. Eldon Epp traces the change (2005). CONTESTED "Prominent among the apostles" or "well known to the apostles."
Priscilla · Acts 18, Romans 16
A tentmaker with her husband Aquila, with a church in their house. When the gifted preacher Apollos arrives knowing only John's baptism, they take him aside and explain "the way of God more accurately." She's named first in four of six mentions.
Lydia · Acts 16
A dealer in purple cloth, a luxury trade, with her own household and no husband mentioned. The first person baptized in Philippi. She insists the missionaries stay with her, and after prison they go back to her house to meet the believers. The patron.

THE PEOPLE OF THE TEXT · WOMEN · XXV–XXX, CONTINUED

The women who ran things, continued

Tabitha · Acts 9
The only person in the New Testament called mathētria, the feminine of "disciple." When she dies, the widows show Peter the garments she'd made. Mutual aid, recorded as ministry.
The widows · Acts 6, 1 Timothy 5
The first recorded dispute in Jerusalem is over Greek-speaking widows neglected in the daily food. 1 Timothy sets up an enrolled order of widows, age sixty and up, with support.
For TKC

POSSIBLE Titles like deacon and patron are rendered as domestic words in some translations, and the Empire's designations do the same on purpose. Tabitha's widows look like Irongate's Kitchen and Meridian's Reading Circles (DOM).

CORRECTION TO THE OLD COMPANION The old companion said Junia was "imprisoned for it," for being an apostle. The text says only that she'd been in prison with Paul.

THE PEOPLE OF THE TEXT · MEN · I–V

The founders

The founders aren't presented as moral exemplars. They're presented, with startling candor, as men whose obedience keeps endangering the people in their care.

Abraham · Genesis 12–25
Held up as the model of faith. The text records him twice passing his wife off as his sister to protect himself, sending Hagar and Ishmael into the wilderness, and taking his son up a mountain with a knife. It also records him bargaining with God over Sodom, from fifty righteous down to ten: "Shall not the Judge of all the earth do what is just?" Paul reads him as justified by faith, James by works. POSSIBLE Dominion preaches the mountain and never the bargaining.
Jacob · Genesis 25–35
Buys his brother's birthright for stew and takes the blessing by disguise. Deceived in turn on his wedding night, he works twenty years under shifting terms. He wrestles all night, is renamed Israel, "strives with God," and limps afterward. He loves Rachel over Leah and Joseph over his brothers, openly. For TKC: the favoritism engine.
Judah · Genesis 37–38, 43–44
Leah's fourth son. He proposes selling Joseph instead of killing him, wrongs Tamar and admits it, and in Egypt offers himself as a slave in Benjamin's place, in the longest speech in Genesis. His name became the tribe's, the kingdom's and finally the word "Jew."
Joseph · Genesis 37–50
Sold, imprisoned on a false accusation, and raised to run Egypt's grain. His famine policy ends with the population selling its land and itself to the crown. "You meant evil against me, but God meant it for good." For TKC: the competent administrator whose competence builds the machine.
Moses · Exodus–Deuteronomy
Raised in the palace of the regime killing his people. He kills an overseer and runs to Midian. He argues with God five times before he goes and later talks God out of destroying the people. He's eighty when he faces Pharaoh. Barred from the land after striking the rock, he dies in sight of it, in a grave no one knows.
CORRECTION TO THE OLD COMPANION The old companion said Moses spent "forty years as a shepherd." Exodus doesn't give the length. The forty years come from Stephen's speech (Acts 7) and later tradition.

THE PEOPLE OF THE TEXT · MEN · VI

David

1 Samuel 16 – 1 Kings 2 · the man every scriptural monarchy has claimed as precedent

Usually reduced to
A boy with a sling, a psalmist with a harp, and "a man after God's own heart." Three images, all from the first third of his story.
What the text gives
The most fully drawn character in the Hebrew Bible, and the portrait isn't flattering. A gifted musician and a brilliant political operator. An outlaw whose men ask Nabal for payment. A king who dances before the ark and quarrels with Michal over it. A man who sees a woman from a roof, sends for her, and has her husband killed by written order. A father who does nothing when his son rapes his daughter, and nearly loses the kingdom to another son. On his deathbed he gives his heir a list of men to deal with.
What Dominion does
POSSIBLE Everything. Divinely chosen, anointed by a prophet, forgiven after private repentance and promised an eternal dynasty (2 Sam 7), the legal basis of every throne that ever called itself Christian. His crimes are taught as a lesson in personal repentance and never as a case for accountability.
TKC echoes
The Emperor, and the founding myth he's measured against. Judah, if he's being trained into a role built on a man whose worst acts are on the record and forgiven in advance.
Where it breaks
A prophet walks into the throne room, tells him what he did, and lives. The scene is the difference between a monarchy with a conscience mechanism and one without. OPEN QUESTION Does anyone in Dorian's court have Nathan's office?

THE PEOPLE OF THE TEXT · MEN · VII–XIII

Kingmakers, prophets and priests

The oldest fight in the collection. The priest is appointed, hereditary and salaried. The prophet is called, unlicensed and unpaid.

Samuel
When the people ask for a king "like all the nations," he warns them what a king will take: sons for his chariots, daughters for his kitchens, the best fields, a tenth of the flocks, "and you shall be his slaves." He anoints one anyway, and later anoints the replacement while the first is still alive. The cleric who makes rulers and outlives them.
Nathan
Delivers the dynastic promise, then tells David the story of the rich man and the poor man's lamb and says "You are the man." He survives, and later works with Bathsheba to put Solomon on the throne.
Solomon
Wins the succession and has rivals killed. His first judgment is between two women who are prostitutes, each claiming a baby, and he hears them both in person. He builds the temple with conscripted labor. 1 Kings 9 says the forced labor fell on non-Israelites, and 1 Kings 12 says the north revolted over his "heavy yoke," so the passages pull against each other. He breaks all four prohibitions of Deuteronomy 17.
Gideon
Offered hereditary rule, he refuses: "The LORD will rule over you." Afterward he makes a gold ephod that becomes a snare. His son Abimelech kills seventy brothers to be king, and the survivor Jotham answers with the fable of the trees, where the olive, fig and vine all decline the crown and the bramble accepts it.

THE PEOPLE OF THE TEXT · MEN · VII–XIII, CONTINUED

Kingmakers, prophets and priests, continued

Elijah
Confronts Ahab and Jezebel and wins on Carmel. Afterward he runs, asks to die, and is fed and told to sleep. God comes to him in "a sound of thin silence." Malachi promises his return. The King James New Testament spells him Elias.
Jeremiah
Tried for his life, his scroll cut up and burned, dropped in a muddy cistern. A Cushite palace servant, Ebed-melech, gets the king's permission and pulls him out with ropes and rags.
Aaron
The first high priest. He makes the calf, announces a feast to the LORD and says the gold went into the fire "and out came this calf." He keeps his office, joins Miriam's challenge and isn't struck.
The structural point for Dominion

POSSIBLE When Dominion folded the prophet into the priest, it didn't abolish prophecy. It made prophecy an employment category, a far more elegant solution.

THE PEOPLE OF THE TEXT · MEN · XIV–XVIII

The men of the New Testament

The men closest to the center are shown getting it wrong again and again, and the record was kept by the movement they went on to lead.

Peter
Rebukes Jesus and is called "Satan." Cuts off a servant's ear in the garden. Denies knowing him three times, the first to a servant girl. Restored over breakfast with a question asked three times. Paul rebukes him in public at Antioch for withdrawing from gentile tables.
Paul
A Pharisee who persecuted the movement and joined it. Imprisoned repeatedly, he writes that in Christ there is "no longer slave or free, no longer male and female," and greets women leaders by name.
Judas
Keeps the common purse. The gospels give different reasons for what he does. Matthew has him ask what they'll pay, John calls him a thief, Luke and John say Satan entered him. Matthew and Acts give two different deaths.
Pilate
The Roman prefect of Judea, 26–36 CE. All four gospels have him find no case and order the execution anyway. Only Matthew has him wash his hands. An inscription found at Caesarea in 1961 names him. For TKC: POSSIBLE: the Crown advisor in the Pairing Introduction (TR) is a Pilate.
John the Baptist
A priest's son preaching in the wilderness, who calls the crowds a "brood of vipers." Herod Antipas has him beheaded at a banquet. The gospels give a personal reason, his objection to Herod's marriage. Josephus gives a political one, Herod's fear of his hold on the crowds.
CORRECTION TO THE OLD COMPANION The old companion said the silencing verses and Galatians 3.28 are "both his." 1 Corinthians 14.34–35 sits elsewhere in some manuscripts and may be a later addition, and 1 Timothy's authorship is CONTESTED. It also said "one gospel implies disillusionment" for Judas. None does.

The novel is named after a line from his prayer. Here's what he meant by it, and what the Empire has decided he meant.

v · For TKC431
JESUS, KINGDOM AND POWER · I

What he meant by kingdom

Start with the word, because everything turns on it. Basileia means kingdom, kingship or reign, and it's the word for the empire that ruled Judea. "The kingdom of God" is the center of his preaching in the first three gospels. Mark's summary is "The time is fulfilled, and the kingdom of God has come near." Matthew usually says "kingdom of heaven," a reverent way of not saying God, and means the same thing. He wasn't inventing a private spiritual category. He was making a claim about who governs, in a province where saying so got people crucified along the roads.

What he poured into that political word runs against what the word normally carries. The parables describe the kingdom as small, hidden and growing without permission: a mustard seed, yeast in flour, treasure buried in a field, a merchant who sells everything for one pearl. It belongs to the poor. Children, tax collectors and prostitutes go in ahead of the respectable, and it's harder for the rich to enter than for a camel to go through a needle's eye. Rank is turned over on purpose. When his followers argue about who's greatest he tells them the rulers of the nations lord it over their subjects, "but it shall not be so among you." In John he washes their feet, a slave's work. When a crowd wants to make him king by force he withdraws. He enters Jerusalem on a donkey. INTERPRETATION Marcus Borg and John Dominic Crossan read the entry as a counter-procession to the Roman governor's arrival with troops at Passover. The charge on the cross, in all four gospels, is "King of the Jews." CONTESTED: Whether the kingdom is present, future or both. Albert Schweitzer argued in 1906 that Jesus expected the end of the world soon, C. H. Dodd argued in 1935 that the kingdom was already present in his ministry, and most scholars now hold some version of "already and not yet." Luke 17.21 says the kingdom is entos hymōn, "within you" or "among you," and the translation turns it from an inner state into a present reality in a community.

The rulers of the nations lord it over them. It shall not be so among you.
MATT 20.25–26
NTKINGDOM
Two sayings the Empire needs
"My kingdom is not of this world" (John 18.36). CONTESTED Ek tou kosmou toutou is "from this world," and many read it as a statement about the kingdom's source more than its location. "Render to Caesar" (Mark 12.13–17) answers a trap about Roman tax. He asks whose image is on the coin. CONTESTED Two spheres, an evasion, or a challenge, since his hearers know whose image human beings bear (Gen 1.27).
MARGINALIA

JESUS, KINGDOM AND POWER · I · WRITING

What Dominion teaches the kingdom is

The relocation

POSSIBLE The Temple empties the word. The kingdom becomes an inner condition of the obedient heart or a reward after death, anywhere but here and never a claim on territory. Having emptied it, the Empire claims the word for itself: Dominion is the kingdom come, which makes resisting the state the same as resisting God. The old companion called this the engine of the novel (AM).
